**The third:** had the one who subtracted confined himself to subtraction, he and the one who added would stand at a single degree. But along with his subtraction he added, by his own opinion, a sentence of the ugliest and feeblest sort — namely, his saying:

> الصَّلَاةُ خَيْرٌ مِنَ النَّوْمِ

Glory be to God! Where is prayer and where is sleep, that the two should be set against each other in terms of better and lesser, like one who says, "This sword is better than wood," not knowing whether he is praising or mocking? So the one who subtracted holds two errors, while the one who adds to the call to prayer holds one, and the two are not equal.

**The fourth:** grant that it was his exercise of judgment. Why then did he command the dropping of the Prophet's phrase from all five prayer times, yet attach his own sentence only once, in the dawn prayer? And how many hideous and feeble things are contained in this sentence — matters I have left aside for fear of length.

As for the ascription of this addition to our master al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah, the blessings of God be upon him — while the Twelver Shia, despite their disagreement with him in school, nonetheless employ this sentence in the call to prayer — it is a plainly void ascription, and whoever makes it speaks in raving slander.

As for his saying that ascribing this subtraction to Umar ibn al-Khattab is unacceptable because it does not exist in the books of those people: his saying carries no weight. Regarding the books that those people rely on for soundness — such as Sahih al-Bukhari and the rest of the five and al-Mishkat — the disagreement of their own scholars over those books' soundness, and the fabricated reports found within them, relieve the people of truth of any need to refute them or to set up proofs against them. Moreover, the narration transmitted by our master al-Qadi al-Numan from our master Jafar al-Sadiq predates the compilation of those people's books; and it may well be that when they saw in it an opening for censure against them, they dropped it and erased it, fearing to give room to the censure of the people of truth — for such has been their custom. This is the refutation of the saying that one who adds to the call to prayer is like one who subtracts from it.

As for the origin of the ascription of this addition, the answer is that this phrase and the testimony to guardianship issued from the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, concerning himself, when the office of guardianship passed to him after the Prophet — just as the Prophet testified for himself to prophethood and messengership, and as God, glorified is He, testified concerning His own essence, saying:

> شَهِدَ اللهُ أَنَّهُ لَا إِلَهَ إِلَّا هُوَ

And that was after the verse of guardianship was revealed concerning him — His saying, "Your guardian is only God and His Messenger," to the end of it. Though they have disagreed about the occasion of its revelation, out of a desire on their part to deny his guardianship, their books are nonetheless filled with testimony that it was revealed concerning the Commander of the Believers, the blessings of God be upon him. In this there is sufficiency for those who seek right direction and guidance; and as for the stubborn disputer, he has nothing but going astray.

> وَكُلُّ إِنَاءٍ يَتَرَشَّحُ بِمَا فِيهِ وَاللهُ يَهْدِي مَنْ يَشَاءُ إِلَى صِرَاطٍ مُسْتَقِيمٍ

Every vessel seeps with what is in it, and God guides whom He wills to a straight path.

## 16. Where a Muslim Stands to Pray

In the book of questions and answers I mentioned at the outset — arranged by one of its compilers in the order of the chapters of *Daaim al-Islam* — I find the matter of mosques treated as follows.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what has come in the Sharia forbidding prayer between graves, the dislike of building a mosque beside them, and the saying of the Messenger of God (ع):

> جُعِلَتْ لِي الْأَرْضُ مَسْجِدًا وَطَهُورًا إِلَّا الْحَمَّامَ وَالْمَقْبَرَةَ وَالْحُشَّ

The earth has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification, except for the bathhouse, the cemetery, and the latrine.

Explain its detail to us. **Answer:** This is in accordance with what has been stated in the outward sense of the Sharia. One does not pray in any of those places at all, and no mosque is built beside graves.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a mosque given over to public use that stands above a house or a shop: may its owners remove it and pull it down, or not? **Answer:** If it belongs to him alone and he has not given it over to public use, he may pull it down. If it has been given over to public use, it is not lawful for him to pull it down; and if it collapses on its own, nothing is owed by him.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a man who wishes to build the congregational mosque or another on his own property: must he seek the governor's permission for that, or not? **Answer:** He does as he wishes and does not seek permission.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what has come in the Book of Purity — that there is no prayer for the neighbor of the mosque except in the mosque, meaning that his prayer does not carry the merit of one who prays in the mosque — and also that the prayer of a man alone in his house is a single prayer? So, our master, what is the difference between the neighbor of the mosque and any other person, if the prayer of a man in his house is a single prayer? **Answer:** The neighbor of the mosque is the one who hears the call to prayer, and the prayer of a man without congregation is a single prayer. For the neighbor of the mosque, however, it is disliked for him to pray alone in his house without an excuse.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what is related in the Book of Purity — that when the mosque of the Prophet (ع) leaked, the people said, "O Messenger of God, if only we plastered it," and he said:

> لَا، عَرِيشٌ كَعَرِيشِ مُوسَىٰ

No; a shelter like the shelter of Moses.

What is the meaning of this saying? **Answer:** It means that this is not like the plastered shelter of Moses.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a man who hears the call to prayer from a mosque in which one of the licensed men of the mission leads the prayer, while the hearer prays in his house with another licensed man: have the two of them forfeited a prayer of merit, or not? **Answer:** The prayer in the mosque is more excellent, as the Prophet (ع) said:

> لَا صَلَاةَ لِجَارِ الْمَسْجِدِ إِلَّا فِي الْمَسْجِدِ

There is no prayer for the neighbor of the mosque except in the mosque.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a mosque in which the imam leads the noon prayer with the congregation, while another imam leads that same prayer with another congregation on the roof: is that permissible, and is the prayer of greeting prayed both below and above, or not? **Answer:** There is no harm in it, and there is no prayer of greeting above it for one who has already prayed it below.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a quarter in which there are several mosques, and a man leaves his house at the time of prayer: may he pass by the nearer mosque and go to the farther one because the licensed man in the farther one is of greater rank than the licensed man of the nearer? **Answer:** Yes, that is established, and there is no blame upon one who does so.

**Question:** What does our master say concerning a mosque that a man sets up within his house but has not dedicated to God, exalted be He, as a place open to the people: may he relocate it or absorb it into an enlargement of his house, and is the prayer of greeting prayed in it, or not? **Answer:** If the mosque is open to the people, the prayer of greeting is prayed in it. If it is confined within the house and has not been made open, it is a place of prayer only — the prayer of greeting is not prayed in it, and he may incorporate it into whatever part of the house he wishes, as long as he has not made it open.

**Question:** What does our master say concerning a man who comes to the mosque at the time of dawn while the imam is praying the obligatory prayer in congregation, and the one arriving is able to pray the Sunnah and still join the imam: does he pray the greeting and the Sunnah, or does he enter with the imam? **Answer:** If it is possible for him to reach the prayer before the imam bows, let him pray whatever he wishes of the Sunnah prayers before the imam raises his head from bowing.

**Question:** What does our master say concerning a man who comes to the mosque before midday to make up an obligatory prayer he has missed: does he pray the greeting, or not? **Answer:** He does not pray a Sunnah prayer while an obligatory prayer is owed by him, except the Sunnah of the dawn, for that has been joined to the obligatory prayers; and the greeting is not prayed except at times when the Sunnah prayer is permitted.

**Question:** What does our master say concerning one who comes while the imam is in the obligatory dawn prayer and enters into it with him: does he make up the greeting or the Sunnah after finishing it, or after midday? **Answer:** If he prays anything before the greeting, its time has passed. There is no harm in making up the Sunnah after the obligatory dawn prayer, as long as the sun has not yet risen — or after midday, or at night.

**Question:** What does our master say concerning an imam who has led the congregation in prayer in a mosque and then departed, after which another licensed man comes: does he lead that same prayer in congregation in that mosque, or not? **Answer:** Two imams do not lead prayer at one time in one assembly, unless the imam is overtaken by something that breaks his state and he appoints another imam in his place.

**Question:** If the imam leads the funeral prayer with the people in the mosque, what is the ruling regarding the greeting? **Answer:** If they entered the mosque at a time when supererogatory prayers are permitted, they pray the greeting first.

Other rulings follow:

- Whoever prays in open country and finds nothing to set up as a screen draws a line and prays; he does not leave his sandal facing the qibla as a screen.
- Whoever prays the doubled prayer or the Sunnah of noon in his house and then comes to the mosque begins with the greeting of the mosque, if his entry is at the time of noon or when dawn has broken.
- Whoever builds a mosque and people are buried around it afterward, prayer in it is permitted; but if it was built after the grave, prayer in it is not permitted.
- It is disliked that the qibla of the mosque face toward a bathhouse, toward graves, or toward a latrine.
- Whoever does not know the qibla because something prevents him exerts his judgment and prays. If he errs and learns of the error while he is still in the prayer and the time is short, he turns toward the direction of the House and completes his prayer, provided he was between the east and the west; but if he had his back to the Kaaba, nothing suffices him but repetition.
- If two directions are confused for a man — so that he sees the qibla as lying in each of them and neither of the two prevails in his judgment — he prays toward both directions together; and the like of this has been mentioned concerning the four directions.
- Whoever travels and the time of prayer comes upon him on the road while he is in a state of major impurity and finds no water, and there is a mosque on the way, performs the dry ablution and prays in the mosque.
- If prayer has been performed in congregation in the mosque and the imam departs and the congregation departs, and a second imam comes with a congregation, that imam prays by himself and they pray by themselves. The leading of prayer is not held in one mosque more than once.
- Whoever enters the mosque, prays the greeting of the mosque, then goes out for a need and returns, prays the greeting of the mosque again.
- A man does not pray with camels facing the qibla; but if they are cattle, sheep, or the like, the prayer is permitted.
- Whoever prays the doubled prayer in his house in its proper time and then enters the mosque may not pray the greeting of the mosque until dawn breaks; then he begins with the greeting of the mosque and prays the dawn prayer. If he enters the mosque and prays the greeting of the mosque and the time of the doubled prayer returns, he repeats the doubled prayer, for there is no prayer between the doubled prayer and the dawn prayer.
- Whoever prays the obligatory prayer in the mosque, and another comes and places a sword facing the qibla, and the worshipper is under a restriction and unable to move away, completes the prayer, and his prayer is valid.
- It is disliked to pray between the two pillars as long as the worshipper finds another place in the mosque; and if he finds none, the prayer is permitted.
- If land lies between partners and one of them builds a mosque upon it, prayer in it is permitted, because he built on his own property; and if the other builds a mosque, prayer in it is permitted also.

From the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf*: It is disliked that the qibla of the mosque face toward a bathhouse, toward graves, or toward a latrine. Whoever does not know the qibla because something prevents him exerts his judgment and prays; if he errs and learns of the error while he is still in the prayer and the time is short, he turns toward the direction of the House and completes his prayer, if it was between the east and the west; but if he had his back to the Kaaba, nothing suffices him but repetition. And if two directions are confused for a man — so that he sees the qibla as lying in each of them and neither of the two prevails in his judgment — he prays toward both directions together; and the like of this has been mentioned concerning the four directions.

From the book *Tawil al-Daaim*: as for His saying, وسلاحكم, the laying down of weapons and the bringing of them into the mosques is not permitted in the outward sense, except for the imam of the mosque on the day of Friday.

It is said in the book *al-Idah* that Abu Abd Allah (ع) disliked that a mosque be inside a house with a door locked upon it.

It is said in *Majalis al-Bayan* that prayer is disliked in twelve places:

1. The cemetery
2. The slaughterhouse
3. The bathhouse
4. The latrine
5. The resting places of camels
6. Prayer facing a sleeping person
7. Prayer without a screen
8. Upon wet, impure ground
9. Upon anything of food
10. Prayer in places of worship
11. Churches
12. The houses of the Magians, unless they have been sprinkled with water

Our master and our lord and our father, Muhammad ibn al-Fahd — may God exalt his sanctity and grant us his intercession and his company — said in his noble epistle, which he named *The Clear Highway of the Path of Guidance for Those Who Seek Guidance, and the Uncoverer of Obscurities in the Juristic Questions of Religion*:

**Question Nineteen:** You asked also — may God, glorified be He, grant you success in what pleases Him — about prayer in a domed structure or a mosque in which there is a grave on any of its sides. **Answer:** You have, in this too, by the granting of success from God, glorified be He, the sound word concerning it, and its truth is this: if the said domed structure or the said mosque was built for prayer to be performed in it, and afterward the making of a grave in it occurred to one who made it there and buried a dead person in it after that, then prayer in either of them is permitted, in its obligatory prayers and its Sunnah prayers. But if either of them was built on account of a grave that required it, coming after the grave, then their ruling is the ruling of the cemetery, and prayer is never permitted in either of them for one who would pray.

He said also — may God exalt his sanctity — in the noble epistle mentioned, concerning the matter of the greeting of the mosque built for the seeking of the face of its Owner:

You also asked — may God, glorified be He, grant you success in all that pleases Him — about the prayer of greeting the mosque: is it obligatory only within the mosque itself, never in its courtyard and forecourt? And if someone enters the mosque and performs it there, then goes out to the courtyard and returns, is he obliged to perform it again on account of that departure, or not?

Here too, with the granting of success from God, glorified be He, you have reached the sound word on the matter. The truth of it is this: the greeting prayer is obligatory only within the mosque itself — not in its courtyard and not in its forecourt. If it is performed in those areas, that is good, since they have become part of the mosque and within its bounds. Therefore, if a person inside the mosque goes out to the courtyard and returns — as I have explained — he is not obliged to repeat it, because he has not yet gone out from the mosque's bounds.

The book *Tawil al-Sharia* records, relating it from the Prophet (ع), that he said:

> لَا تُقَامُ الْحُدُودُ فِي الْمَسَاجِدِ، وَمَنْ سَمِعْتُمُوهُ يُنْشِدُ ضَالَّتَهُ فِي مَسْجِدٍ فَقُولُوا: لا وجدت

The prescribed penalties are not to be carried out in the mosques; and whoever you hear calling out for his lost property in a mosque, say to him: May you not find it.

The same book records that the Prophet (ع) said:

> لَا بَأْسَ بِالصَّلَاةِ فِي مَرَابِضِ الْغَنَمِ

There is no harm in praying in the folds of sheep.

The author of *al-Majalis al-Mustansiriyya* — may the peace of God be upon its author — said in the eighth assembly: Hear now what we set before you concerning its meaning: prayer has seven ranks, by which the prayers of those who pray are graded one above another:

1. the prayer of the worshipper in his house, as a single prayer;
2. the prayer of the worshipper in the mosque of the market, as twelve prayers;
3. the prayer of the worshipper in the mosque of the tribe, as twenty-five prayers;
4. the prayer of the worshipper in the congregational mosque, as one hundred prayers;
5. the prayer of the worshipper in the Farthest Mosque, as one thousand prayers;
6. the prayer of the worshipper in the mosque of the Messenger of God, as ten thousand prayers;
7. the prayer of the worshipper in the Sacred House of God, as one hundred thousand prayers.

For every one of these there are abundant meanings and likenesses of wisdom.

Our lord, the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said in his four hundred rules of conduct:

> الْجُلُوسُ فِي الْمَسْجِدِ بَعْدَ طُلُوعِ الْفَجْرِ إِلَىٰ طُلُوعِ الشَّمْسِ أَسْرَعُ لِطَلَبِ الرِّزْقِ مِنَ الضَّرْبِ فِي الْأَرْضِ

Sitting in the mosque from the breaking of dawn until the rising of the sun is أسرع for seeking provision than traveling إِلَى الْأَرْضِ.

And he said in it: The Muslim is not to spit toward the qibla; and if he does so forgetfully, let him ask forgiveness. And he said in it: A man is not to blow upon the place of his prostration, nor upon his food, nor upon his incense. And he said in it:

> ولا يصلي أحدكم وبين يديه سيف، فإن القبلة آمن

None of you is to pray with a sword before him, for the qibla is a place of safety.

And he said in it: Whoever eats any of the offensive foods, let him not come near the mosque.

## 17. Who May Lead, and Who May Not

I come now to the subject of the imamate.

I begin with the book of questions and answers I named at the outset, which its compiler arranged according to the order of the chapters of دَعَائِمُ الْإِسْلَامِ.

**Question:** What do you say concerning an imam who prays with the people in the arch of the qibla while they stand behind him in the public mosque, given that the common people dislike the imam's prayer in the arch, though not his prostration in it?

**Answer:** There is no harm in praying and prostrating in it.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a traveler who follows a resident in a four-cycle prayer and prays two cycles with him? Is it better for him to give the greeting of peace and depart, or to remain until he gives the greeting together with the imam at the end of the cycles?

**Answer:** It is better for him to remain until he gives the greeting together with the imam.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what has come in the Shariah forbidding prayer behind the محدود? O our master, what is meant here by the حد and by the one so described?

**Answer:** The محدود is one upon whom the prescribed penalty has been carried out.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what has come in the Shariah, that the one in fetters does not lead those who are free of them? O our master, does this refer to every kind of bond and shackle placed upon him, or only those upon the legs?

**Answer:** What is meant is bonds and shackles upon the legs and elsewhere.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what has come in the Shariah, that there is no harm in praying behind a slave if he is learned in jurisprudence and there is none present more learned than he to lead his people? O our master, does "his people" mean those like him among the slaves, or all who are below him in rank?

**Answer:** He leads whoever is below him in rank, even if they are free men.

**Question:** What do you say concerning one granted license who prays with the people while some of the common people are with him, and who therefore omits saying رَبَّنَا لَكَ الْحَمْدُ out of taqiyya? Does a deficiency enter his prayer or not?

**Answer:** There is no harm in it for the sake of taqiyya, but he should not omit it for any cause other than taqiyya. Rather, let him use it as the pure friends of God used it.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a ship — broad, long, and wide — in which an imam prays the noon prayer with the people in one part of it, and then another imam prays that same prayer in another part, and likewise in a spacious house? Is that permitted or not?

**Answer:** If there is a partition between them, there is no harm. Otherwise, two imams are not to be gathered in one place, unless the later of them is the more excellent. The meaning of "gathering" here is this: one granted license prays the noon prayer with the people in a house or elsewhere, and then one above him among those granted license comes and prays that same prayer with another congregation in that same house with no partition between them. That is permitted to him.

**Question:** What do you say concerning an imam who prays with the people on a ship while seated — because of the movement of the ship, the narrowness of the place, or some other cause that makes standing impossible? Is that permitted or not?

**Answer:** There is no harm in it for necessity, whatever the necessity may be.

**Question:** What do you say concerning an imam who prays with the people and stands ahead of them by a span or so, while all those following him have taken their place on his right and have left his left side empty, and so they prayed? Is their prayer valid or not?

**Answer:** They may pray on his right or on his left, and there is no harm in his standing slightly ahead. Their prayer is sound.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a man who prays the obligatory sunset prayer with the people while the people of the outward have also gathered with him? How is he to recite بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ aloud, when among them that is not permitted at all?

**Answer:** He must recite بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ aloud, but he may lower his voice so that he hears himself only, for the sake of taqiyya.

**Question:** What do you say concerning one granted license who prays with the people, and certain letters do not come out of his mouth correctly — either because his teeth have fallen out or because he knows little of the points of articulation? Is it permitted to complete the prayer with him or not?

**Answer:** If he alters anything of the meaning, the prayer is spoiled.

**Question:** What do you say concerning one granted license who prays with the people and whose nose bleeds in the midst of his prayer? May he appoint in his place, to complete the prayer, one whom he sees as fit for it — though that person is not among those to whom leave has been given to appoint — or is the prayer left void?

**Answer:** If he is one granted unrestricted leave, there is no harm, and he may put forward whom he wishes.

**Question:** What does our master say concerning a mosque in which an imam prays the noon prayer with the people, and when they have departed another one granted license comes and prays that same prayer there with the people — they not knowing of the first prayer, and then afterward coming to know? Are they ordered to repeat the prayer or not?

**Answer:** The prayer has been completed and there is no repetition required of them, whether they knew of it or did not know.

> فَخُذْ مَا آتَيْتُهُ وَكُنْ مِنَ الشَّاكِرِينَ

So take what you have been given, and be among the grateful.

**Question:** What do you say? May a resident pray with travelers, and a traveler with residents?

**Answer:** If the traveler is more excellent than the residents, he leads them and departs after two cycles, and they rise and complete the prayer. Likewise, if the resident is more excellent than the travelers, he leads them and they depart after two cycles.

**Question:** Concerning an imam who prays with a congregation and, when he has finished, recalls that there was an impurity upon him which he had known of before the prayer and had forgotten to wash away — does the one who prayed with the imam repeat both the obligatory prayer and the sunna, or is nothing required of him but the repetition of the obligatory prayer without the sunna?

**Answer:** Nothing is required of the follower but the repetition of the obligatory prayer. As for the sunnas, no.

From another source: if a eunuch prays with women it is permitted, and his sitting with them is permitted.

From the book مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ: if a man opens an obligatory prayer for himself, wherever he may be, and then an imam comes and steps forward, it is recommended that the man bow and prostrate until he completes that cycle, count it as a supererogatory prayer for himself, and begin the prayer anew with the imam. If he does not do so and continues his prayer alone, that suffices him. If some of those following stand in front of the imam, the prayer of the imam is valid, and so is the prayer of whoever stands at his side, but the prayer of whoever stands in front of him is not valid.

When prayer is held in Mecca and the rows encircle the Kaaba, no row may draw closer to the Kaaba's walls than the imam's position, from any direction.

If one who has not yet reached maturity holds the office of appointed governor over the Muslims, no one may stand ahead of him in prayer — though the imamate of other boys is not permitted in the obligatory prayers.

One whose recitation is so distorted that it alters the meanings of the Quran may not step forward to lead. Nor may one whose deafness prevents him from hearing when he is corrected.

When the master of a slave is present in the slave's own dwelling, the master holds the stronger right to the imamate.

The book of the interpretation of the Shariah of our master al-Muizz records, on the authority of the Prophet (ع), that he once rode a horse that stumbled beneath him; he fell, and his right side was injured. His companions came to visit him. When the time of prayer arrived, he led them in prayer while seated. They rose to stand, and he signaled to them to sit — so they sat. When he had completed his prayer, he said:

> إِنَّمَا الْإِمَامُ لِيُؤْتَمَّ بِهِ فَإِذَا كَبَّرَ فَكَبِّرُوا فَإِذَا قَرَأَ فَأَنْصِتُوا وَإِذَا قَالَ سَمِعَ اللَّهُ لِمَنْ حَمِدَهُ فَقُولُوا رَبَّنَا لَكَ الْحَمْدُ اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ وَإِذَا سَلَّمَ فَسَلِّمُوا وَإِذَا صَلَّى جَالِسًا فَصَلُّوا جُلُوسًا أَجْمَعِينَ

The author of the book of glosses states that اَلْاِخْتِصَارُ permits the imamate of a blind man when there is need, though he is not to be taken as a standing imam. اَلْمُنْتَخَبَةُ further states that a blind man may not lead unless he is set straight on a given day, that he is not to be a standing imam, and that the nasib is set aside if he has led them from the side.

Our master al-Qadi al-Numan ibn Muhammad, in an epistle of his responding to questions put to him by some of the judges, wrote: "You asked about the imamate of the blind man. There is no harm in his leading if he is set straight, but he is not to be taken as a standing imam."

The book مُخْتَصَرُ الْآثَارِ records: "We have related from the People of the House (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that they disliked the imamate of the one afflicted with white leprosy, the leper, the child of adultery, the castrated, the owned slave, and the blind man — unless he is set straight when there is need, in which case he is not to be taken as a standing imam. Likewise, they did not hold that the bedouin should lead, nor the one upon whom the prescribed penalty has been carried out, nor the madman." It is further stated there that license was given to these persons to lead one another, each one of them leading whoever is in a condition like his own.

The book مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ states: if a man prays with a congregation and his ablution is then broken, and he exits the mosque without appointing anyone to complete the prayer with them, their prayer is spoiled. If they put someone forward after the imam has left the mosque, it does not suffice them. It suffices only if someone is put forward at the very moment the imam's ablution breaks, before he exits the mosque. Furthermore, no follower may begin any act of the prayer before the imam; he must begin after him. If he precedes the imam in anything through forgetfulness, he returns to his prior position so as to remain a follower.

The book الْمَجَالِسُ الْحَاتِمِيَّةُ relates, on the authority of the Prophet (ع), that he said:

> إِنَّمَا يَخْشَى الَّذِي يَرْفَعُ رَأْسَهُ قَبْلَ الْإِمَامِ أَنْ يَمْسَخَ اللَّهُ رَأْسَهُ رَأْسَ حِمَارٍ

The one who raises his head before the imam has cause to fear that God will transform his head into the head of a donkey.

In the book of purity it is stated: if a wall stands between the imam and those following him, it is not permitted for them to follow him unless they can see his prayer — either by the wall being low enough that they can see his prayer over it, or by the wall being pierced with openings, as the walls of the women's enclosures in mosques are made.

In the book مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ it is stated: if a man leads a congregation in prayer and two latecomers join him, having missed part of it, then when the imam gives the greeting of peace they rise to complete what remains due from them, and one of them follows the other — the prayer of the one leading is valid, but the prayer of the one following is spoiled.

It is related in the book الإيضاح that the Messenger of God (ع) combined the noon and afternoon prayers, and the sunset and later evening prayers, with neither travel nor illness nor any excuse.

Our master, the author of the book الْمَجَالِسُ الْمُسْتَنْصِرِيَّةُ, states in the tenth of its assemblies that the right to lead prayer belongs to seven, ranked one above another in precedence. Our master Jafar ibn Muhammad al-Sadiq (ع) said:

> يؤم القوم أقدمهم هجرة فان استووا فأقرأهم فان استووا في حفظ القرآن فأفقههم وان استووا في علم الفقه فأكبرهم سناً وصاحب المسجد أحق بمسجده والإمام عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ ومن أقامه أحق من جميعهم والمرأة تصلي بالنساء ولا تتقدمهن بل يقفن عن يمينها وشمالها

Twelve among the men do not lead in prayer:

1. A sick man does not lead the healthy.
2. Nor does a leper.
3. Nor one afflicted with white leprosy.
4. Nor a madman.
5. Nor one upon whom a prescribed penalty has been carried out.
6. Nor the child of adultery.
7. A bedouin does not lead the emigrants.
8. Nor does one in fetters lead those who are free.
9. Nor does one who has performed the dry ablution lead those who have performed the ablution with water.
10. Nor does the mute lead those who speak.
11. Nor does a traveler lead residents.
12. Nor does one set apart by hostility to the household lead those who follow it.

Each of these may lead whoever shares his own condition.

It is said in the book of questions — which is from the most excellent Shaykh Shamun ibn Muhammad Ghuri al-Hindi (رض) — with the answers from our master Badr al-Din Hasan ibn Idris ibn al-Hasan.

Question: What do you say concerning two men, one holding license in Rajpur and the other in Jalalpur, who met in Rajpur not knowing which was the superior and which the lesser, so that the one licensed in Rajpur led while the one licensed in Jalalpur followed him — and then they met in the same way in Jalalpur, and the one licensed in Jalalpur led while the one licensed in Rajpur followed him? What is the condition of their prayer? Answer: When it is not known which of them is the more excellent, the owner of the mosque has the better right to his own mosque.

Question: What do you say concerning a licensed man who stood to pray for himself, and another man came — whether before the bow or after he had bowed — and followed him, while the licensed man did not know he was leading anyone? Is the prayer valid? Answer: His prayer is valid together with the leader, even though he did not intend to lead.

Question: What do you say concerning an imam who prays with the people on a ship while seated, because the ship's motion or some other cause makes standing impossible for some of them, while the rest remain able? What is the condition of their prayer? Answer: A concession has been granted in this on account of the ship, when they are unable. Whoever is able should make haste and pray alone, standing.

It is said among the questions put by certain of the shaykhs of India, with the answers from certain of our masters, the summoners of Yemen.

Question: If a man prays and with him is a woman of his unmarriageable kin, where does the man stand and where does the woman? Answer: If he prays with a woman of his unmarriageable kin, he stands ahead of her and she prays behind him, or at his right but set back from him. If she is a stranger to him, her prayer alongside him is not valid unless a third person is with them.

A further question, from among the questions of certain of the ranks of India, with its answer from certain of our masters, the summoners of Yemen: You asked about an imam who leads a people while not reciting well, bringing substitution and distortion into his recitation — as when he says أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ بَرِىٓءٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلْمُشْرِكِينَ وَرَسُولُهُۥ with a kasra upon the lam, or وَمِنْهُمْ مَنْ هَدَى اللَّهُ with a fatha upon the ha, and the like. Answer: Whoever does not recite well in such matters may not be taken as an imam. It is incumbent upon him to correct his recitation so that he does not exchange a verse of mercy for a verse of punishment, nor exchange truth for falsehood. It is not fitting for anyone to pray in the prayer of such a man. And upon whoever recites well it is incumbent to pray with the people when they call him to it and he is acceptable to them.

It is said among the questions of the law whose answers are from certain of our masters: You asked about an imam who led a people and then did not know how much of the prayer he had completed or how much remained. He must build upon what is certain. If those behind him prompt him with words of glorification, he returns to the point to which they have prompted him until he arrives at what is correct, provided those behind him are certain of it. If they too do not know, and neither he nor they can determine whether he has added to the prayer or fallen short in it, he begins the prayer anew.

I turn now to the mention of the congregation and the rows, again from that book of questions and answers arranged according to the order of the chapters of دَعَائِمُ الْإِسْلَامِ.

Question: What do you say concerning a man who prays alone while his wife — or a woman of his unmarriageable kin — prays by herself at his right, opposite him; and concerning a man who prays while his wife, or such a woman, stands ahead of him? Is his prayer valid or not? Answer: It is not permitted for the woman to stand ahead of the man and pray while he prays behind her.

Question: What do you say concerning a man of the people of the summons who has prayed the afternoon prayer and then prays that same prayer a second time, outside its time, for the sake of taqiyya — and some of the people of the summons follow him in taqiyya before those who are not upon the way of the People of the House (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), following him and praying behind him with him as their intended imam? What is the condition of their prayer, and does anything fall upon the imam in that? Answer: He does it for the sake of taqiyya, and he need not concern himself with their following him; no sin falls upon him in that.

Question: What do you say concerning what has come in the book of purity — that the Messenger of God (ع) came upon a man who prayed alone behind the rows with a gap before him and ordered him to repeat the prayer, and that Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said the like? O our master, is one who prays in that manner ordered to repeat? Answer: He is indeed ordered to repeat, and this is the established practice.

Question: What do you say concerning a man engaged in the prayer of glorification or another supererogatory prayer? May he cut it short if he fears that the congregation will pass him by? Answer: The obligatory prayer in congregation is more excellent and more binding than any other.

Question: What do you say concerning a man who wishes to pray in congregation and has already entered upon a supererogatory prayer? May he cut it short if he fears that he will miss the performance of the sunna? Answer: If he fears that the obligatory prayer will pass him by, he cuts it short; otherwise he does not.

Question: What do you say concerning a man who entered the sanctified state for the obligatory prayer and had begun his recitation, and then the imam came forward while the man was still standing — and when the imam bowed, he bowed with him without renewing the sanctified state? What is the condition of his prayer? Answer: He must repeat it.

Question: What do you say concerning a licensed man who stood to pray for himself, and another man came — before the bow or after he had bowed — and followed him, while the licensed man did not intend to lead anyone? Is the follower's prayer valid or not? Answer: His prayer is valid together with the leader, even though he did not intend to lead.

Question: If a man prays and with him is a woman of his unmarriageable kin — or he prays with both a man and such a woman — where does the man stand and where does the woman? Answer: If he prays with a woman of his unmarriageable kin, he stands ahead of her and she prays behind him, or at his right but set back from him. If she is a stranger to him, her prayer is not valid unless a third person is with them.

Question: May those holding license read the benefits of religion to women of unmarriageable kin and to others in a single gathering? Answer: If there is a screen between the man and the woman, he reads to her in such a way that she hears his speech but he does not see her. And let no man be alone with a woman unless another woman is with her, or another man with him.

Question: Concerning a man who reaches the imam in the obligatory prayer at the final sitting of witness — does he intend to complete the prayer with him or not? Answer: If he reaches him at the final sitting of witness, he intends the prayer as a follower, for the final sitting of witness is the last of the prayer's limits. He sits and supplicates with the supplication of turning toward God, and when the imam gives the greeting of peace he rises and prays — and he has caught the congregation.

From another source, the saying of al-Sadiq Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع):

> يَنْبَغِي لِلصُّفُوفِ أَنْ تَكُونَ تَامَّةً مُتَوَاصِلَةً بَعْضُهَا إِلَى بَعْضٍ فَيَكُونَ بَيْنَ كُلِّ صَفَّيْنِ قَدْرُ مَسْقَطِ جَسَدِ الْإِنْسَانِ إِذَا سَجَدَ فَاِنْ لَمْ يُصَلُّوا مِثْلَ هَذَا لَمْ تَكُنْ لَهُمْ صَلَاةٌ

By "the fall of a man's body" I mean simply that each worshipper must have sufficient room to prostrate.

If the imam leads the congregation and all the rows are full, and a man arrives to find no place among them, he prays alone alongside the imam — he does not join the imam's own row. But if the imam's hujja, who stands in the imam's place after him, arrives and likewise finds no place in the rows, he steps forward and stands level with the imam and prays with him. This is permitted to no one else. That is the explicit text.

It is said in the book مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ, concerning the saying:

> مَنْ صَلَّى الصَّلَاةَ فِي جَمَاعَةٍ فَظُنُّوا بِهِ كُلَّ خَيْرٍ

Whoever prays the prayer in congregation, think every good of him.

And it is said: this applies when nothing evil is known of him. And it is said in the reports that scholars differed concerning the forming of rows between the pillars — some related that it is disliked, while others held that the position permitting it is the stronger. And it has come in the book الإيضاح that the Messenger of God (ع) joined the noon and afternoon prayers, and the sunset and the later evening prayers, with neither travel nor illness nor any excuse.

Our master Jafar ibn Sayyidna Sulayman answered — from the island of his Yemen to the island of his India — questions put to him by the author of this book, Sayyidna 'Ali ibn Sulayman: That which you asked about, may God preserve you, concerning the women, when they pray in congregation with men from behind a screen — whether it is incumbent upon the one of them nearest to the men to look toward them or not, and whether, if the place is connected, there is any need for them to look toward the men or not. Answer: It is incumbent upon them to look toward the men before entering the sanctified state, just as the Daudis hold that the woman whose place is near the men prays in a place close to them and keeps her gaze upon the men during the bowing and the prostration, and that otherwise there is no prayer for any of them. The intent is that if the place is connected — as through an opening or a lattice — the prayer is thereby complete, and looking toward the men before the sanctified state or after it is not permitted. Indeed, the prohibition of it has come even outside the prayer — so how much more within it, which is the greatest of all acts of worship. And looking during the bowing or the prostration, or while standing or sitting, after the sanctified state has been entered, cuts off the prayer if it is directed toward any of those who are not of unmarriageable kin, for in that is disobedience. If the place is not connected, then the woman nearest the men glances at their garments — one glance only — before entering the sanctified state, and in that is sufficiency; by it the prayer of all of them is completed.

It is said in the interpretation of the Shariah that the Prophet (ع) saw a man praying alone and ordered him to repeat the prayer — meaning he was behind the row without standing level with the imam, facing him from behind. And it is said there: just as none may step forward to the prayer niche except one whom the imam designates when his ablution is broken, so too none of the respondents may step forward except one whom the summoner designates and deems fit for it.

## 18. The Shape of the Prayer Itself

I turn now to the characteristics of the prayer and its sunnahs.

In the question-and-answer book mentioned at the outset — arranged by one of its compilers according to the chapter order of *Daaim al-Islam* — the following exchange appears. Question: What do you say concerning what has come in the Book of Purity, that Ibn al-Kawwa repeatedly opposed Amir al-Muminin Ali (ع) by raising his voice during the prayer, and that Ali (ع) then recited:

> فَٱصْبِرْ إِنَّ وَعْدَ ٱللَّهِ حَقٌّۭ

So be patient; the promise of God is true.

O our master, did Ali (ع) begin the prayer with the surah of which this verse is a part, or not? Answer: No. Rather, he recited this verse in order to silence him and to rebuke him.

Question: What do you say concerning what has come in the books of the Shariah regarding the long portions of al-mufassal, its middle portions, and its short portions? O our master, what are their limits in the madhhab of the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ)? Answer: The long portions of al-mufassal run from al-Hujurat to al-Mujadila; its middle portions run from al-Mujadila to عَم يَتَسَآءَلُونَ; and its short portions run from عَم يَتَسَآءَلُونَ to al-Nas.

From the same source: whoever abandons the prayer without excuse, or abandons any of the prescribed works, has broken the covenant. Whoever accuses a believer, and the accused then swears by God, and the accuser does not believe him, has broken the covenant. Whoever commits fornication or kills a believer has broken the covenant. Whoever reviles the summoner, or reviles the one whom the summoner has appointed, has broken the covenant. If the summoner commands the believer to strive with his person and his wealth, and he refuses without excuse, he has broken the covenant. Whoever forms the intention for the prayer, and then that intention slips away before he enters the prayer — whether through preoccupation, forgetfulness, or anything else — and he then prays after that, his prayer does not suffice him.

It is not permissible for a man to recite within himself what he has not uttered with his tongue. Whoever performs the first tashahhud is not thereby excused from the second, though the second does suffice in place of the first.

He said in the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf*: whoever forms the intention for the prayer, and then that intention slips away before he enters it — whether through preoccupation, forgetfulness, or anything else — and he then prays after that, his prayer does not suffice him. And from the same book: the one praying makes the tawajjuh after the takbirat al-ihram, the imam and the follower being equal in this, so long as the imam has not preceded the follower in something; and he begins the recitation after the tawajjuh.

He said in *al-Ikhtisar*: he opens the prayer with a takbir in which he raises his hands, spreading his palms and joining his fingers in a straight extension. The least by which the prayer is completed — its minimum — is this:

1. Opening with the takbir
2. Standing, for whoever is able
3. Bowing
4. Prostration
5. Sitting for the tashahhud

Whoever performs these five limits, having forgotten or not knowing what lies beyond them, his prayer is complete. But whoever knows their limits and then deliberately omits something from the obligations or the sunnahs of the prayer has no prayer, and he must repeat what he omitted of the prayer in this way. As for the one who is ignorant of this, it is upon him to learn.

He said in *al-Akhbar*: the woman places her hands above her knees upon her thighs, so that she does not stoop too far and thereby raise her hindquarters.

He said in the book *Tawil al-Daaim*: those behind the imam, when he says:

> سَمِعَ اللَّهُ لِمَنْ حَمِدَهُ

God has heard the one who praised Him.

say quietly:

> رَبَّنَا لَكَ الْحَمْدُ

Our Lord, to You belongs all praise.

The exception is the one who relays for the imam when those praying behind him are many and one among them has been appointed to make them hear on his behalf. That man raises his voice with it and with the takbir, but he does not raise his voice with the tasbih.

He said in the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf*: whoever performs the first tashahhud is not thereby excused from the second, though the second suffices in place of the first. And he said in the book *Tawil al-Daaim*, concerning the outward sense, that when the one praying sits in the prayer he folds his left leg beneath him and keeps his right leg upright.

He said in the brief discourse on jurisprudence, whose questions come from the most eminent Shaykh Hasan ibn Mawla Adam al-Hindi and whose answers come from the sayyids of Yemen, may God sanctify all their souls: the prayer of a woman who has not undergone khafd is valid, and intimacy with her is permissible, and likewise for the man; but the sunnah is to hasten to that.

Our master and our lord Jafar ibn Sulayman, may God exalt his sanctity and grant us his intercession and his company, said in his noble epistle, which he entitled *al-Sayf al-Aqta fi Ibtal al-Bida*, with which he counseled his people and roused from heedlessness those among them whose sleep had grown long — the people of the island of Yemen — after he had settled among them and taken up residence there. Section: And of this also is what has been known and witnessed of many among you, men and women alike, who cling to the mountain of loyalty and come to the rope of obedience to God, glorified be He, by way of undertaking the obligation of the prayer, and yet their foreheads do not prostrate to God, glorified be He, in a single rakah of the prayer, so that the prayer is diminished of the rakahs that belong around it. This is among the gravest of all matters in its consequence, and the most notorious of them all in repute. Has there never reached the ears of such people the word of the Messenger of God (ع):

> بَيْنَ الْعَبْدِ وَبَيْنَ الْكُفْرِ تَرْكُ الصَّلَاةِ

and the word of Amir al-Muminin (ع):

> لَا حَظَّ فِي الْإِسْلَامِ لِمَنْ تَرَكَ الصَّلَاةَ

There is no portion in Islam for the one who abandons the prayer.

Given this — that one who abandons the prayer holds no share in Islam, and that abandoning the prayer is precisely what separates the servant from unbelief — no person who neglects the prayer should, while still counting himself among the faithful, suppose that he stands among the Muslims, or that he holds of Islam so much as the parings of a fingernail. God, glorified be He, has declared this in the verses of His clear Book:

> ومن يبتع غير الإسلام ديناً فلن يقبل منه وهو في الآخرة من الخاسرين

And whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers.

Among the people of this description we have seen, those who abandon the prayer fall into two kinds:

- The first are people of tribal temper who have turned away from the concerns of religion altogether, people absorbed in themselves and removed from all of that — the animal nature dominant over them, as though they had sprung from the well.
- The second are people of arrogance and coarse insolence, the women among them most especially.

But there is no insolence and no arrogance against God, glorified be He, nor against His worship. Whoever takes that path has destroyed his own soul and wronged it. As for his Creator, glorified be He, He has no need of that person's worship. Its benefit returns only to the worshipper himself, and to those like him — the learned among them and the lowly. God did not create them and require their service in His worship except to benefit them by it, as God, glorified and exalted be He, has said:

> وَمَا خَلَقْتُ ٱلْجِنَّ وَٱلْإِنسَ إِلَّا لِيَعْبُدُونِ

And I did not create the jinn and mankind except that they should worship Me.

There is no doubt that whoever walks that path has entered into the warning God, exalted be He, issues in the verses of His clear Book, where He says:

> إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِى سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ

Indeed, those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell abased.

It was on that very path that Iblis met his end — and he is truly dead — for whenever a servant prostrates to his Lord, glorified be He, in his prayer, Iblis cries out: Woe to me, this one obeyed and I refused. As for the unbeliever, even though he does not pray the prayer of Islam or worship his Lord, glorified be He, with the worship that is accepted, he still has within his own religion a form of worship that he performs, handed down to him from his forebears. Not even the unbelievers of the lands of India are so arrogant toward the worship of God, glorified be He, nor do they turn away from Him with such turning away — men and women, the whole and the parts — except for those among them who fall under the two kinds already described and who follow them in their misguidance and deviation. Those two kinds are found in all the schools of Islam as well, and likewise in the creeds of every religion. But such conduct does not befit one who has attached himself to the walaya of the friends of God, glorified be He, by its firm mountain, and who traces himself, in his affairs, back to the people of piety and faith. No one has any claim of walaya to them except by holding fast to the hem of the prayer. Otherwise he is like one who proclaims upon himself, in the Hereafter, destruction and woe — like those who oppose the guiding call. Even if there are among them, men and women alike, some who are formed that way, leave them and what they are formed in, for they are not granted success in their affairs, while you, in your affairs, by the praise of God, glorified be He, are granted success. As for the one who acknowledges the call of the friend of God (ع), and the Garden and the Fire, the punishment and the reckoning, the balance and the path, the gathering and the raising — his reason requires him to hold fast to the worship of his Lord, glorified be He, through the prayer, which is the pillar of the religion and the support of Islam, and to comply through it with the command. He who warns has given excuse, and he who has cautioned against the punishment of his Lord has cleared himself. So let those who would set things right set their souls right by this, and whoever fears God and guards himself against Him — those are the ones who prosper.

He said in the book *Tawil al-Shariah*: The obligations of the prayer are seven:

1. The first takbir
2. The recitation
3. The bowing
4. The prostration
5. The tasbih
6. The greeting
7. The taslim

And he said in it: When you have made the takbir, it is not permissible for you to move your foot, to advance it or draw it back; rather you remain fixed. Nor may you speak from the opening takbir until the taslim, nor move from your place.

And he said in it: The Prophet (ع) said:

> أُمِرْتُ أَنْ أَسْجُدَ عَلَى سَبْعَةِ أَدَوَاتٍ

I have been commanded to prostrate upon seven members.

And he said in it: The Prophet (ع) said:

> لا تقرأ وأنت راكع ولا تحركوا الحصى في الصلاة ولا تلتفتوا التّغات الثعلب ولا تقعوا اقعاء القرد ولا تنقروا نقر الغراب

Do not recite while you are bowing; do not disturb the pebbles during the prayer; do not turn about as the fox turns; do not squat as the monkey squats; and do not peck as the crow pecks.

He said in it: He separates the two rakahs with a sitting. He said in it: Do not pray the obligatory prayer when the adhan is called — pray it when the iqama is given. He said in it: The Zuhr prayer is four rakahs. He said in it: It is related from the Prophet (ع) that he said:

> لَا تُطْرِقُوا فِي الرُّكُوعِ إِطْرَاقَ الْحِمَارِ وَالْكُبَابَهِ

Do not droop the head in the bowing with the drooping of the donkey and of the lowered beast.

He commanded, further, that the head not be dropped below the level of the back. He said in it: The head and the back are level and aligned in the prayer. He said in it: The Prophet (ع) said:

> مَنْ كَثُرَتْ صَلَاتُهُ بِاللَّيْلِ حَسُنَ وَجْهُهُ بِالنَّهَارِ

Whoever prays much by night, his face is made beautiful by day.

The author of the guarded epistle, which contains the protected secrets of the religion of God — the most eminent and noble Dai Zayd ibn Muhammad ibn al-Fadl, may God exalt his sanctity and grant us his intercession and his company — said: The obligations of the prayer are seven, and they are:

1. The intention
2. The first takbir
3. The standing
4. The recitation
5. The bowing
6. The prostration
7. The taslim

He said in it: Five prayers have been appointed for every day. He said in it: The prayer has four limits, and they are:

1. The place
2. The time
3. The purity
4. The intention

He said in it: The five prayers are seventeen rakahs. He said in it: The prayer is of two kinds — obligation and sunnah. He said in it: For that reason Muhammad (ع) said:

> تَارِكُ سُنَّتِي مَلْعُونٌ

The one who abandons my sunnah is cursed.

Meaning: the one who abandons my legatee is one banished from the mercy of God. He said in it: The Messenger of God (ع) said:

> لَا صَلَاةَ لِمَنْ لَا يُؤَدِّي الزَّكَاةَ

There is no prayer for the one who does not pay the zakat.

He said in it: The movements of the prayer — the bowing and the prostration — are submission and humility. Then he said: For that reason the Messenger of God (ع) said:

> مَنْ لَمْ تَنْهَ صَلَاتُهُ عَنِ الْفَحْشَاءِ وَالْمُنْكَرِ لَمْ يَزْدَدْ مِنَ اللَّهِ اِلَّا بُعْدًا

Whoever is not restrained by his prayer from indecency and wrongdoing increases in nothing but distance from God.

And for that reason, the people of Shuayb said to him:

> يا شعيب أصلواتك تأمرك أن نترك ما يعبد آبائنا

O Shuayb, does your prayer command you that we abandon what our fathers worshipped?

Then he said: it is because the outward prayer neither forbids nor commands.

Our master, the author of the *Majalis al-Mustansiriyya* — may the peace of God be upon its author — said in the sixth of these councils: You have heard what we recited to you of the divisions of the obligations of purity and its sunnahs, corresponding in number to the clauses of the shahada and its letters, since purity is one of the rights of the shahada. Among its rights also is the prayer. So listen now to what we recite to you of its meaning. The prayers are seven obligatory prayers and twelve sunnah prayers. The obligatory prayers are:

1. The prayer of residence, which is seventeen rakahs in every day and night
2. The prayer of travel, which is eleven rakahs in the day and night
3. The Friday prayer, which is not complete until four gather with the imam
4. The prayer of fear, part of which is prayed with the imam and the remainder of which is completed alone
5. The prayer of the circumambulation of the obligatory Hajj
6. The prayer of the circumambulation of the obligatory Umrah
7. The prayer over the bier, which is an obligation upon every Muslim who knows of it — until one of them undertakes it, whereupon the rest become volunteers in it

The sunnah prayers are:

1. The prayer of the Feast of the Breaking of the Fast
2. The prayer of the Feast of the Sacrifice
3. The prayer of the eclipse of the sun
4. The prayer of the eclipse of the moon
5. The prayer for rain
6. The prayer of the earthquake
7. The prayer for events that occur as signs
8. The prayer of the shaf and the witr
9. The prayer of the watira, which is two rakahs performed sitting
10. The prayer of the two rakahs of the dawn — for the Prophet (ع) made them up after the rising of the sun and made up the obligatory prayer after them
11. Twenty-eight rakahs in every day and night, among them:
    - The prayer of the oft-returning, which is six rakahs after the sun's decline
    - Eight rakahs between Zuhr and Asr
    - Ten rakahs between Maghrib and the later Isha
    - Four rakahs after the later Isha, which is the prayer of the night
12. The prayer of greeting the mosque upon entering it

Then he said, may the good pleasure of God be upon him, in the seventh of these councils: So listen now to what we recite to you — that in each of the five prayers there are seven obligations and twelve sunnahs. The seven obligations are:

1. The intention
2. Turning toward the qibla
3. Then the takbirat al-ihram
4. The recitation of the Opening of the Book
5. The bowing
6. The prostration
7. The sitting for the salutation

The twelve sunnahs are:

1. The adhan
2. The iqama
3. Saying, I have turned my face to Him who created the heavens and the earth, as one of pure faith, to the end of the words
4. Seeking refuge from the accursed Satan
5. Reciting a surah after the Opening of the Book in the first two rakahs of every obligatory prayer
6. Raising the hands at every rising from the bowing and every descent into it
7. The tasbih during the bowing
8. The imam's saying, God has heard the one who praised Him, and the follower's saying, Our Lord, to You belongs all praise
9. The tasbih during the prostration
10. The first sitting and the first tashahhud
11. The second tashahhud
12. Every prayer in which there is no first sitting has a qunut that stands in its place in the count, and every prayer in which there are two sittings for the tashahhud has no qunut in it

Our lord Amir al-Muminin Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) said in his Four Hundred Etiquettes:

> لَا يَجْمَعُ الْمُؤْمِنُ يَدَيْهِ فِي الصَّلَاةِ وَهُوَ قَائِمٌ يُشْبِهُ بِأَهْلِ الْكِتَابِ

The believer does not clasp his hands together in الصلاة while standing, resembling thereby the People of the Book.

And he said in it:

> إِذَا أَصَابَ أَحَدُكُمْ فِي الصَّلَاةِ الدَّابَّةَ فَلْيَدْفِنْهَا أَوْ لِيَسْتُرْ عَلَيْهَا أَوْ لِيَضَعْهَا فِي ثَوْبِهِ حَتَّى يَنْصَرِفَ

If أحدكم of you comes upon أصاب a crawling creature during the prayer, let him bury it, أو cover it over, or place it in his garment until he has finished.

And he said in it:

> الْاِلْتِفَاتُ الْفَاحِشُ يَقْطَعُ الصَّلَاةَ فَمَنْ فَعَلَهُ فَعَلَيْهِ الْاِبْتِدَاءُ . اَلصَّلَاةُ بِالْأَذَانِ وَالْإِقَامَةِ وَالتَّكْبِيرِ

Gross turning about cuts off the prayer, and whoever does it must begin again: the prayer with the adhan, the iqama, and the takbir.

And he said in it:

> أَعْطُوا كُلَّ سُورَةٍ حَقَّهَا مِنَ الرُّكُوعِ وَالسُّجُودِ إِذَا كُنْتُمْ فِي الصَّلَاةِ

Give every surah its due of bowing and prostration when you are in the prayer.

And he said in it:

> أَجْلِسُوا بَيْنَ السَّجْدَتَيْنِ حَتَّى تَسْكُنَ جَوَارِحُكُمْ وَقُومُوا فَاِنَّ ذَلِكَ مِنْ فِعْلِنَا

Sit between the two prostrations until your limbs are still, then rise — for that is our practice.

And he said in it:

> لِيَرْفَعِ السَّاجِدُ مُؤَخَّرَهُ فِي الصَّلَاةِ إِذَا سَجَدَ

Let the one who prostrates raise his haunches when he prostrates.

And he said in it:

> إِذَا صَلَّيْتَ وَحْدَكَ فَاَسْمِعْ نَفْسَكَ الْقِرَاءَةَ وَالتَّسْبِيحَ وَالتَّكْبِيرَ وَإِذَا انْقَّلْتَ عَنِ الصَّلَاةِ فَعَنْ يَمِينِكَ

When you pray alone, make yourself hear your recitation, your tasbih, and your takbir. When you turn away from the prayer, turn to your right.

A question was raised by the head of the island of India, Muhammad Ali ibn Salih al-Badri, may God preserve him, and answered by the lord and master, our master Hibat Allah, may God prolong his life. The following is its text and substance.

The second question: What does our master — may God perpetuate his support — say concerning a person who recites Surat al-Falaq and Surat al-Nas inadvertently in the obligatory prayer? Does he complete the prayer, or does he repeat it? And if it is permissible, what is the ruling for one who recited Surat al-Nas in the first rakah? Does he repeat it in the second, or does he recite the surah before it from its opening, or does he repeat the prayer? And if it is not permissible, what of one who recited Surat al-Ikhlas in the first rakah? Does he repeat it in the second, or does he recite the surah above it from its opening? May our master — may God perpetuate his exaltation — favor us with a satisfying answer.

Answer: We seek help from God for success and right direction, and from His friend and his full moon we ask for rain from the clouds of confirmation. No text has come from the friends of God forbidding the recitation of the two surahs of refuge in the obligatory prayer. So if the one praying recites them, his prayer is complete. If he recited Surat al-Nas in the first rakah, he repeats it in the last. Whoever recited Surat al-Ikhlas in the first recites al-Falaq in the second. One should not, however, deliberately recite in this manner in the obligatory prayer, placing it in the position of the sunnah.

## 19. Supplication, Speech, and Silence

I turn now to supplication after the prayer.

In the question-and-answer book arranged according to the chapters of the Pillars of Islam, the question was put: what is to be said about the teaching found in the books of the guiding call — that supplication is not recited after the afternoon prayer? Does this mean only the supplication of drawing near, or all supplications? The answer given was: both the supplication of drawing near and all others — and if anyone does supplicate, no sin rests upon him.

A further question was asked: does the worshipper supplicate after the obligatory afternoon prayer, and does he wipe his face at the place of prostration at the close of every prayer? The answer: it is not permitted except after the afternoon prayer and after dawn. Our masters, may God sanctify their souls, commanded wiping specifically after the afternoon prayer and after dawn, and this is among what they received from the pure Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). It has been noted that one does not prolong supplication after the afternoon prayer, yet if he does prolong it, nothing rests upon him.

The book on the interpretation of the Sharia states that when the prayer falls in the daytime the recitation is kept low; when it falls at night, in the dominion of darkness, the voice is raised; and the dawn prayer, offered in the last darkness of night, is announced aloud.

Our lord and master, the author of the Mustansiriyya Councils, peace of God be upon him, said in the Eleventh Council: having brought the discussion of the jurisprudence of prayer to the limit set out above — where the outward receives its exposition and its expoundings, and the inward its sign and its intimation — we follow it with what is now read to you: the mention of the supplication that comes at the close of the prayer, and what it contains of the seven and the twelve, in the manner our explanation has proceeded throughout, for the benefit of those of understanding who take it to heart.

Of the seven, these are they:

1. the supplication of asking (دُعَاءُ الْمَسْأَلَةِ), in which the supplicant spreads out his hands and sets the inner surface of his palms facing his face;
2. the supplication of seeking refuge (دُعَاءُ الْاِسْتِعَاذَةِ) from what he fears, in which he turns his palms about and sets their backs facing his face;
3. the supplication of humble submission (دُعَاءُ الْاِسْتِكَانَةِ), in which he grasps his shoulders with his hands — this is done only in solitude;
4. the supplication of devotion (دُعَاءُ التَّبَتُّلِ), in which he points with the index finger;
5. the supplication of entreaty (دُعَاءُ التَّضَرُّعِ), which is made by moving the finger;
6. the supplication of fervent appeal (دُعَاءُ الْاِبْتِهَالِ), in which he spreads his palms and extends both hands together, raised toward the sky;
7. the supplication of abasement in the dust (دُعَاءُ التَّعْفِيرِ), in which he supplicates while turning his cheeks upon the ground.

For each of these seven supplications there is a mention in the Quran, which we shall bring forward when we return to the explanation of what we have read, for everything we have set down is of the nature of roots — like the roots of a tree awaiting the branching of its boughs and the bearing of its fruit.

As for the twelve, they are the foundations of supplication and the base upon which it is built. Whoever desires that his supplication be answered should do as follows:

1. send ahead of it repentance from what God has forbidden;
2. or a charity that he gives;
3. or a fast;
4. or a prayer — for God, mighty and majestic, says:

> إِلَيْهِ يَصْعَدُ ٱلْكَلِمُ ٱلطَّيِّبُ وَٱلْعَمَلُ ٱلصَّلِحُ يَرْفَعُهُۚۥ

To Him ascends the good word, and the righteous deed raises it;

5. then purify his intention in the supplication and turn toward God with his whole heart;
6. begin with the praise of God, exalted is He, and with extolling Him as He deserves;
7. call down blessings upon the Prophet at the beginning of the supplication, in its middle, and at its end;
8. draw near to God, exalted is He, through the imams of the religion, naming them one by one until he reaches the imam of his own age;
9. seek access through him and through them to God in the answering of his supplication;
10. then ask for the good of this world and the hereafter;
11. seek refuge from the evil of this world and the hereafter;
12. then supplicate after that for whatever he loves and wills.

Our master, the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said in his Four Hundred Etiquettes:

> وَاذْكُرُوا اللَّهَ عَزَّ وَجَلَّ يَقْبَلْ دُعَاءَكُمْ عِنْدَ ذِكْرِهِ وَحِفْظِكُمْ وَرِعَايَتِكُمْ إِيَّاهُ

Remember God, mighty and majestic — He will accept your supplication when you remember Him, guard His right, and keep watch over it.

And he said:

> تَقَدَّمُوا فِي الدُّعَاءِ قَبْلَ نُزُولِ الْبَلَاءِ فَإِنَّ أَبْوَابَ السَّمَاءِ تُفَتَّحُ فِي سِتَّةِ مَوَاقِيتَ: ١- عِنْدَ نُزُولِ الْغَيْثِ ٢- وَعِنْدِ الزَّحْفِ ٣- وَعِنْدَ الْأَذَانِ ٤- وَعِنْدَ قِرَاءَةِ الْقُرْآنِ ٥- وَعِنْدَ زَوَالِ الشَّمْسِ ٦- وَعِنْدَ طُلُوعِ الْفَجْرِ

Come forward with supplication before affliction descends, for the gates of heaven are opened at six appointed times:

1 – at the falling of rain;
2 – at the advance of the ranks in battle;
3 – at the call to prayer;
4 – at the recitation of the Quran;
5 – at the declining of the sun;
6 – at the breaking of dawn.

And he said:

> الدُّعَاءُ يَرُدُّ الْقَضَاءَ الْمُبْرَمَ فَادْعُوهُ وَاسْتَعْجِلُوهُ

Supplication turns back the decree that has been firmly settled, so call upon Him and press your request with urgency.

And he said:

> ادفعوا داعي البلاء بالدعاء وعليكم به قبل نزول البلاء فوالذي فلق الحبة وبرأ النسمة للبلاء أسرع إلى المؤمن من السيل من أعلى الجبل إلى أسفلها ومن ركض البراذين

Repel the summons of affliction through supplication, and hold fast to it before affliction descends. By Him who split the seed and brought the soul into being — affliction overtakes the believer more swiftly than a torrent down a mountainside, and more swiftly than the galloping of horses.

And he said:

> اَلدَّاعِيُ بِلَا عَمَلٍ كَالرَّامِي بِلَا وَتَرٍ

Whoever supplicates without deeds is like an archer with no bowstring.

And he said:

> مَنْ قَرَأَ قُلْ هُوَ اللَّهُ أَحَدٌ إِلَى أَنْ تَطْلُعَ الشَّمْسُ عَشْرَ مَرَّاتٍ وَإِنَّا أَنْزَلْنَاهُ فِي لَيْلَةِ الْقَدْرِ لَمْ يُصِبْ ذَنْبًا وَلَوْ اجْتَهَدَ إِبْلِيسُ

Whoever recites until sunrise the chapter beginning "Say: He is God, One" ten times, and the chapter beginning "Indeed We sent it down on the Night of Decree" — no sin will touch him, however hard Iblis may strive against him.

I turn now to the subject of speech and of acts performed within the prayer.

It was asked in that same book of questions and answers: what is to be said of a man praying behind the imam when the imam loses his place, and the man alerts him by clearing his throat or by some similar means rather than by words of glorification — does anything fall upon him for that? The answer: clearing the throat cuts off the prayer when it is done deliberately and without excuse. He should alert the imam with words of glorification, or with a gesture.

It was asked: what is to be said of a man who clears his throat often in prayer out of mere habit — does anything fall upon him for that? The answer: if he does so deliberately, he must repeat the prayer.

It was asked: what is to be said of a woman who prays — whether in congregation or alone — while carrying her child, because there is no other way to quiet his weeping — is that permitted to her or not? The answer: it is not permitted to her to occupy herself in prayer with such a thing.

It was asked: what is to be said of one who prays with food in his mouth, placed there on account of pain — does a deficiency enter his prayer on that account or not? The answer: if he placed it there out of necessity, nothing falls upon him.

It was asked: what is to be said of a man who prays with his braid wound inside his turban, drawn from the back of his neck around to the region of the forehead above the hairline, without tucking it into the middle of his head — is that permitted or not? The answer: there is no harm in that. The binding up of hair that is forbidden is when a man turns the hair back upon its root, gathering it to the middle of his head, and the like of that.

As for a man who prays with something in his mouth resembling a gemstone or a dirham, or anything of that kind — if it does not prevent him from forming the letters properly, his prayer is complete. If he holds something in his hand that does not prevent him from fulfilling the limits of his prayer, nothing falls upon him. Yet to be free of all such things is more fitting for him.

The abridgment of the compiled book states that whoever eats or drinks in prayer — whether forgetfully or deliberately — has cut off his prayer.

The book of the interpretation of the Sharia states that the recitation comes before the bowing. And it says:

> وَلَا تَرْفَعْ صَوْتَكَ فَوْقَ صَوْتِهِ

Do not raise your voice above his — meaning the voice of the imam.

It also says that the recitation in the noon prayer and the afternoon prayer is not made aloud but kept low, because these belong to the prayers of the daytime. And it says that in the night prayer the recitation is made aloud. And it says that the Prophet forbade wiping over the leather socks and forbade omitting the audible utterance of the words "In the name of God, the Merciful, the Compassionate."

> الْمُصَلِّي إِذَا كَبَّرَ لَا يَتَكَلَّمُ حَتَّى يُسَلِّمَ

Once the worshipper has pronounced the opening magnification, he does not speak until he gives the salutation.

Our master Jafar ibn Mansur al-Yaman, may God exalt his sanctity, said in his book on the inward nursing, concerning the meaning of prayer: the prayer has doors that are numbered, and their full number must be completed, for by them the prayer is made complete. They are:

1. the ablution;
2. the opening;
3. the call to prayer;
4. the standing call;
5. the turning toward God;
6. the recitation;
7. the bowing;
8. the praise;
9. the glorification;
10. the prostration;
11. the testimony;
12. the salutation;
13. the supplication of the standing invocation;
14. the supplication of the odd-numbered prayer.

These are fourteen doors from which the worshipper has no escape. They are the supporting pillars of the prayer, and without them his prayer is not complete.

## 20. What the Worshipper Wears and Stands Upon

I turn now to the dress of the worshipper and what he prostrates upon.

In the book of questions and answers mentioned at the outset — the one whose arranger organized it according to the chapters of the book دَعَائِمُ الْإِسْلَامِ (Pillars of Islam) — I find the following exchanges.

Question: What is your ruling on the prayer mat, the rug, and other cloths on which the qibla or another image is depicted, in such a way that it does not occupy the worshipper when he looks at it? Is prayer upon such items permitted, whether the image falls in the place of prostration or elsewhere?

Answer: If it is of a kind that occupies the worshipper when he looks at it, prayer is not permitted. If it does not occupy him, prayer is permitted — except in the case of images, toward which he does not pray.

Question: What is your ruling on a man who prays in a garment dyed with safflower that is not deeply red? Is his prayer permitted? And what of a woman who prays in pure silk, or in a garment of a very deep red, whether dyed with safflower or not? Is her prayer permitted?

Answer: This belongs to the domain of etiquette. If anyone wears such a garment, it does not invalidate the prayer performed in it, whether the wearer is a man or a woman — except for pure silk, which is disliked for men.

Question: What is your ruling on what has come in the books of the Sharia — that if any part of a woman's عورة ('awra) becomes exposed during prayer her prayer is spoiled, whether she knew of it or not? The women of the people of the dawa in India do not customarily wear the long shift and the head-veil, and their legs are uncovered during prayer once or twice. How then does their prayer stand?

Answer: Covering is indispensable, for the whole of a woman is عورة. If any part of her awra is exposed, prayer is not permitted to her, whether she knew this ruling or was ignorant of it. This is its meaning.

Question: Concerning the man wading through water, the man in fear, and the traveler pressing hard upon his journey — men who carry weaponry that bars them from prayer, such as the feathering of arrows, leather straps, the trimmings of daggers, and garments of pure silk — is their prayer complete without repetition, or not?

Answer: Prayer is valid only in pure garments. Whatever is impure, the worshipper strips from himself.

Question: What is your ruling on a man who prays while carrying weights of gold and silver upon him, wrought or unwrought, tucked into his waistband? Is his prayer established or not?

Answer: His prayer is complete and established.

Question: The abridgment of the Musannaf states that there is no harm in praying upon a rug bearing figures, so long as they are not in the place of prostration and do not divert him from the prayer. Is prayer upon such a rug permitted? And likewise, if garments bearing figures are worn, is prayer in them permitted?

Answer: As for the rug and the garment bearing figures, prayer is not permitted when they are seen. When they are not seen, prayer in them and upon them is permitted.

Question: If the worshipper prays upon stone, upon unbaked brick, or upon the like of these, is prayer upon them permitted or not?

Answer: Obligatory prayer upon them is not permitted; only the supererogatory and the sunna prayer.

Question: What is your ruling on a man who applies henna to his hand or foot for adornment and not for any benefit? Is that permitted?

Answer: It is not fitting that men use henna for adornment. Women are commanded to use it. For men, to leave it aside without cause is better, though no sin falls upon one who does it.

From another place: prayer is permitted in the garments of the children of Muslims and the slaves of Muslims, so long as no impurity appears in them and none is seen, when they themselves do not pray. Whoever prays, then finishes his prayer, and then sees that his garment was impure — if he knew of it beforehand, his prayer is void and he repeats it, whether its time has passed or not. A man is not to pray bareheaded, without turban and without mantle; but if he finds neither, he prays. Whoever prays leaving upon his shoulders only a small kerchief — that is, his handcloth — while he has another garment available, his prayer is not permitted; but if he finds nothing else, his prayer in it is permitted.

The book of the abridgment of the Musannaf states: if a man prays with his عَوْرَة uncovered and does not know of it until he has finished the prayer, he must repeat it once he comes to know. And if a free woman or a slave woman prays with her awra uncovered — whether she knew of it or not — her prayer is spoiled.

Our master Jafar ibn Mansur al-Yaman, may God exalt his sanctity, says in the book of the obligations and the limits of religion: it is lawful for a man to uncover his head openly, to go out among the congregation, and to pray with his head uncovered. If a man prays with something in his mouth — such as a gemstone, a dirham, or the like — and it does not prevent him from forming the letters properly, his prayer is complete.
