If he is holding something in his hand and it does not prevent him from fulfilling the limits of his prayer, there is no harm upon him; yet to be free of it is better for him.

From the same source: whoever has nothing but an impure garment, can obtain no other, and has no water with which to purify it, prays in it and repeats the prayer when he finds another garment or water to purify it. If he has two garments, one of them impure, but does not know which one, it is incumbent upon him to pray that prayer in each of them, intending by it the performance of the obligation. There is no harm in praying upon a rug bearing figures, so long as they are not in the place of prostration and do not divert him from the prayer. As for plaster and the like, over which carrion is burned and with which the mosque and the house are plastered, there is no harm in prostrating upon it, for the fire has purified it.

In the book of the interpretation of the Daaim, upon the saying:

> وَلَا بَأْسَ فِي الصَّلَاةِ فِي الْقَمِيصِ الْوَاحِدِ الْكَثِيفِ إِذَا أَزَرَّهُ

There is no harm in praying in a single thick shirt if he belts it — meaning the worshipper. The sense of this is that, when he bows, his private part should not appear from its edge.

From the same, upon the saying:

> مَثَلُ جَنَاحَيِ الْخُطَّافِ

Like the two wings of the swallow. This means a garment that he wraps over his back and whose two ends he brings back upon his shoulders, when he finds nothing other than that.

From the same, upon the saying:

> نَهَىٰ عَنِ اشْتِمَالِ الصَّمَّاءِ

He forbade the wrapping called اِشْتِمَالُ الصَّمَّاءِ. That is when a man wraps himself in a single garment, setting its middle upon his head or upon his shoulders, drawing one end with his left hand against his body, and turning the end on his right toward his left and behind him, gathering it with his right hand, so that he becomes as though sewn into it with both his hands beneath it. If he prays in this condition, he cannot perform the prayer properly: he cannot set his palms upon the ground as required, he cannot bow or prostrate, and he cannot manage the raising of his hands. Such a prayer is therefore incomplete and does not suffice him.

The book of the abridgment of the Musannaf states: as for the slave woman, the woman under a contract of manumission, the woman freed at her master's death, and the girl who has not yet reached menstruation, there is no harm in their praying without a head covering. Whichever of them is set free while she does not know of it must repeat every prayer she prayed after her freedom without a head covering.

The book of questions and answers — whose questions come from the most venerable Shaykh Yusuf ibn Ismail ibn Ibrahim al-Samati al-Hindi and whose answers come from our master Idris ibn al-Hasan, may God exalt his sanctity — contains the following. Question: Is prayer permitted upon stones and plaster? Favor us with a clear and sufficient answer. Answer: The Prophet said:

> جُعِلَتْ لِي الْأَرْضُ مَسْجِدًا وَطَهُورًا

The earth has been made for me a place of prostration and a means of purification.

And everything of the earth with which dry ablution is not permitted — prayer upon it is not permitted.

The book of questions on matters of the Sharia and their answers — containing one hundred and three questions, put by certain eminent shaykhs of India and answered by certain callers from Yemen, may God sanctify their souls — records the following.

**The seventeenth question:** Concerning a free woman who prays with a net upon her head — does the net serve her in place of a mantle, or not? **Answer:** It is not recorded that prayer in a shift and head-veil suffices unless both are thick; and the net does not cover.

**The fourth question:** Concerning camels, cattle, and sheep that feed upon filth — when their milk and their clarified butter, or the egg whites of hens, reach the garments or bodies of those praying, do they render them impure or not? **Answer:** A small amount of it is permitted.

It is said in the book of the interpretation of the Sharia: there is no prayer except upon the earth or upon what grows from it, such as green plants. And it is said therein: the Prophet said:

> حُبِّبَ إِلَيَّ مِنْ دُنْيَاكُمْ ثَلَاثَةٌ النِّسَاءُ وَالطِّيبُ وَجُعِلَتْ قُرَّةُ عَيْنِي فِي الصَّلَاةِ

Three things of your world have been made beloved to me: women and perfume, and the delight of my eye has been placed in prayer.

And it is said therein: the Prophet (ع) said:

> كُلُّ صَلَاةٍ لَا يُقْرَأُ فِيهَا بِفَاتِحَةِ الْكِتَابِ فَهِيَ خِدَاجٌ غَيْرُ تَامَّةٍ

Every prayer in which the Opening of the Book is not recited is defective and incomplete.

And it is said therein:

> لَا صَلَاةَ فِي أَعْطَانِ الْإِبِلِ وَلَا بَأْسَ بِالصَّلَاةِ فِي مَرَابِضِ الْغَنَمِ بَعْدَ أَنْ تُكْنَسَ وَتُرَشَّ

Prayer is not valid in the resting places of camels. There is, however, no harm in praying in the folds of sheep once they have been swept and sprinkled with water.

Our master the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said in his four hundred counsels of etiquette:

> لَا يَقُومَنَّ أَحَدُكُمْ بَيْنَ يَدَيِ اللَّهِ عَزَّ وَجَلَّ اِلَّا وَعَلَيْهِ ثَوْبٌ نَظِيفٌ

Let none of you stand before God, the Mighty and Exalted, except in a clean garment.

And he said therein:

> لا يصلي الرجل في قميص متوشحاً فأنه فعل قوم لوط

A man is not to pray in a shirt draped loosely across him, for that is the practice of the people of Lot.

And he said therein:

> لَا تَجُوزُ الصَّلَاةُ فِي ثَوْبٍ يُعَسْطِلُ فِيهِ عَلَى عُنُقِهِ وَفِي الْقَمِيِّصِ الصَّفِيقِ يَرُدُّهُ عَلَيْهِ

Prayer is not permitted in a garment that hangs slack about his neck, nor in a close-woven shirt unless he draws it over himself.

And he said therein:

> لَا يَسْجُدُ الرَّجُلُ عَلَى صُورَةٍ وَلَا عَلَى بِسَاطٍ هِيَ فِيهِ

A man must not prostrate upon an image, nor upon a rug that contains one.

And he said therein:

> وَلَا يَعْقِدُ الرَّجُلُ الدِّرْهَمَ الَّذِي فِيهِ الصُّورَةُ فِي ثَوْبِهِ وَهُوَ يُصَلِّي وَتَجُوزُ أَنْ تَكُونَ الدَّرَاهِمُ فِي هِمْيَانٍ أَوْ ثَوْبٍ إِذَا كَانَ طَاهِرًا وَلَا يَسْجُدُ الرَّجُلُ عَلَى كُدْسِ الْحِنْطَةِ وَلَا عَلَى شَعِيرٍ وَلَا عَلَى شَيْءٍ مِمَّا يُؤْكَلُ وَلَا عَلَى الْخُبْزِ

A man must not knot a dirham bearing an image into his garment while praying. It is, however, permitted for coins to be carried in a money belt or in a cloth, provided it is clean. He must not prostrate upon a heap of wheat, barley, anything edible, or bread.

## 21. Friday and the Two Festivals

### On the Friday Prayer

I turn now to the Friday prayer, and I set down first what I find in the book of questions and answers mentioned at the outset — which one of its arrangers ordered according to the chapters of *Daaim al-Islam*.

The question: What do you say concerning what has come in the Book of Purity — that if three miles or more separate two congregational mosques, Friday prayer is established in each of them, whether they stand in one city or two; but if the distance between them in a single city falls short of that, Friday prayer is established in only one of them? Our master, if the distance is less than three miles and the two mosques belong to two separate cities, is Friday prayer established in both of them or not?

The answer: Friday prayer is not established in both when the distance between them is less than three miles, whether they belong to one city or to two.

The question concerns the words of our master al-Muayyad in the Friday sermon:

> فَمَنْ تَرَكَهَا مُتَعَمِّداً أَوْ مُسْتَخِفّاً بِحَقِّهَا مِنْ غَيْرِ عُذْرٍ لَهُ مَعَ إِمَامٍ عَدْلٍ أَوْ جَائِرٍ فَلَا تَجْمَعِ اللهُ شَمْلَهُ

Whoever abandons it deliberately, or makes light of its due, without excuse — whether under a just imam or an unjust one — may God not gather his affairs together; to the end of the passage.

The answer: It is not permissible to pray behind the people of injustice except for the sake of dissimulation, as has come in the Shariah from Mawlana al-Baqir Muhammad ibn Ali (ع), who said:

Do not count the prayer behind a الناصب or a الحروري as valid. Treat such a person as one of the pillars of المسجد and recite for yourself, as though you were praying alone.

> لَا تَعْتَدَّ بِالصَّلَاةِ خَلْفَ النَّاصِبِ وَلَا الْحَرُورِيِّ وَاجْعَلْهُ سَارِيَةً مِنْ سَوَارِي الْمَسْجِدِ وَاقْرَأْ لِنَفْسِكَ كَأَنَّكَ وَحْدَكَ

The commentary on the reports records that al-Husayn ibn Ali (ع) put the governor of Muawiya forward to lead the prayer over his brother al-Hasan ibn Ali (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), and said:

> لَوْلَا السُّنَّةُ لَمَا قَدَّمْتُكَ

Were it not for the practice, I would not have put you forward.

By that, he meant the practice of the People of the House (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) in dissimulation — as one of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) said:

> التَّقِيَّةُ دِينِي وَدِينُ آبَائِي

Dissimulation is my religion and the religion of my fathers.

As for what is meant by "the unjust" in his words, it refers to whoever has been set up among the summoners and from whom injustice and transgression have appeared: it is obligatory to pray behind him, and it is incumbent upon the people, when they observe this in him, to bring the matter before the holder of authority and to await his response. This is the meaning of his saying:

> أَيُّهَا الصَّالِحُونَ فَتَحَقَّقُوا ذَلِكَ وَلَا تَرْتَابُوا بَعْدَ الْيَقِينِ فَقَدِ اعْتَصَمْتُمْ بِجَبَلِ اللَّهِ الْمَتِينِ

O righteous ones, verify this and do not fall into doubt after certainty, for you have taken firm hold of the strong mountain of God.

He says in the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf*: The Friday prayer behind the Imam of the Muslims is more excellent than the prayer behind his deputies. If the Imam is delayed because he is occupied with some of the interests of the Muslims, and his deputies pray in his stead in mosques that are near one another, then the prayer in his own mosque — the one in which it has been his custom to pray — is more fitting than in any other.

He says in it: The call to prayer that obliges the one upon whom the Friday duty rests to leave off buying and selling is the call given in the presence of the preacher when he sits in the place from which he delivers the sermon. If two of those obliged to the Friday prayer transact a sale at the time when it is forbidden, their sale is not annulled. Whoever grows thirsty while the Imam is on the pulpit may drink.

He says in it: If the Imam delivers the sermon on Friday and his state of purity is broken, he descends and performs the ablution; then if he wishes he repeats the sermon, and if he wishes he prays — all of this suffices him. If he delivers the sermon seated because of an ailment, that suffices him. It is disliked for him to omit the sitting between the two sermons, but if he has done so, repetition is not obligatory upon him.

If the Imam delivers the sermon on Friday and then some matter overtakes the people and they go off to it, and only one man remains with him, then he prays four cycles. If he has delivered the sermon to the people on Friday and prayed one cycle with them and then they all departed and he remained alone, he prays the Friday prayer as two cycles. If he has opened the prayer and they departed before he bowed, he begins afresh the noon prayer of four cycles. If the Imam prays the Friday prayer with the people, and when he has finished the second cycle he rises to stand and then remembers, it is upon him to sit, to make the testimony, to give the greeting of peace, and to perform the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

And from it: Whoever among the people of Friday sleeps while waiting for the Friday prayer, and the prayer is established and purification is not possible for him, performs the dry ablution and prays with the Imam, then repeats it as four.

He says in the book of reports: And he leans upon his sword; if he does not find a sword, then upon a staff.

He says in the book *Tawil al-Shariah*: The sermon is before the prayer, and the prayer after the sermon is two cycles. He divides the sermon into two portions with a sitting that separates them. The raising of the voice in its recitation distinguishes it from the noon prayer on the remaining days, and the noon prayer is at seven hours. He said: The call to stand for prayer comes after the sermon.

He says in it: The practice of the respondents in the assembly of the summoner is like the practice of the congregation on Friday, or the congregation behind the Imam. When the preacher on Friday ascends the pulpit and faces the people, silence becomes obligatory, such that it is not permissible for anyone to speak or to occupy himself with anything other than listening to him and giving ear to him.

He says in it: It is not permissible for him to give suck unless his summoner has appointed him to the suckling and chosen him for it. When the Imam recites, listening becomes obligatory and nothing else, and it is not permissible to turn away from him. Likewise, it is not permissible for the respondent to press proposals upon the summoner or to demand from him the various branches of knowledge, for he knows better how to raise his children.

Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad ibn al-Fadl said in his *Hidden Epistle*: The Friday prayer points to Muhammad (ع), who is the gate of the Garden, and he has two summonses — an outward summons to the outward face of his Shariah and an inward summons to the interpretation of his law. Then he said: Because the prayer on the remaining days was four cycles, on Friday it became two, and the sermon was made a substitute for the two cycles. It is praise, admonition, supplication, and exposition from the Lord of exposition, and it is never without warning. The sitting during the sermon is the separation between the upper light and the lower light; the Qaim (ع) rises with the two lights. Then he said: The preacher is not to deliver the sermon upon the call to prayer that has already been given, until the muezzin who stands before the seat gives the call after the Imam has ascended the pulpit. Then he said: The call is given only in the congregational mosque, as an announcement. Then he said: Whoever gives the call on that day in one of the mosques other than the congregational mosque has erred, and if he did it deliberately he is punished and commanded to perform the washing of Friday. Then he said:

> فَاعْرِفْ فَضْلَ يَوْمِ الْجُمُعَةِ حَتَّىٰ يَجْمَعَ اللَّهُ شَمْلَكَ بِيَوْمِ السَّبْتِ فَتَسْتَرِيحَ وَتَسْبُتَ وَلَا تَشْقَىٰ إِنْ شَاءَ اللَّهُ تَعَالَىٰ

Know the excellence of the day of Friday, so that God may gather your affairs together on the day of the Sabbath and you may rest and repose, spared from wretchedness — if God the Exalted wills.

Mawlana Amir al-Muminin Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) said in his Four Hundred Points of Conduct:

> مَنْ كَانَتْ لَهُ إِلَى اللَّهِ حَاجَةٌ فَلْيَطْلُبْهَا فِي ثَلَاثِ سَاعَاتٍ: ١- فِي يَوْمِ الْجُمُعَةِ سَاعَةَ زَوَالِ الشَّمْسِ ٢- وَحِينَ تَهبُّ الرِّيَاحُ وَتُفْتَحُ أَبْوَابُ السَّمَاءِ وَتَنْزِلُ الرَّحْمَةُ ٣- وَسَاعَةً فِي آخِرِ اللَّيْلِ عِنْدَ طُلُوعِ الْفَجْرِ.

> وَإِنَّ مَلَكَيْنِ يُنَادِيَانِ هَلْ مِنْ تَائِبٍ فَيُتَابَ عَلَيْهِ هَلْ مِنْ سَائِلٍ فَيُعْطَىٰ هَلْ مِنْ مُسْتَغْفِرٍ فَيُغْفَرَ لَهُ هَلْ مِنْ طَالِبِ حَاجَةٍ فَأَجِيبُوا دَاعِيَ اللَّهِ

Whoever has a need before God, let him seek it in three hours:

1. On the day of Friday, at the hour when the sun passes its zenith.
2. When the winds blow, and the gates of heaven are opened, and mercy descends.
3. An hour at the end of the night, at the breaking of dawn.

He says in it: the supplication of devotion in the Friday prayer falls before the bowing in the second cycle. The recitation on Friday is: in the first cycle, the chapter of the Congregation after the Opening of the Book, and in the second, the Opening of the Book followed by the chapter of the Hypocrites.

He says in it, among legal questions that are all marvels, another question: What do you say of a preacher who was delivering the sermon on Friday when a man entered through the door of the congregational mosque, whereupon the sermon of that preacher was spoiled, and so was the prayer of those who prayed with him? The answer: that preacher is the property of the man who entered through the door of the congregational mosque, for that man had appointed him as his deputy; so when he came in upon him, it was no longer permissible for him to go before his master, nor to lead him in prayer.

### On the Prayer of the Two Festivals

I likewise set down here what is contained in the book of questions and answers mentioned at the outset, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *Daaim al-Islam*.

The question: What do you say concerning what has come in the Shariah — that no tashriq is incumbent upon the traveler except in a congregational city? Our master, is what is intended by tashriq here the festival prayer, or not? And whoever forgets one of the magnifications in the festival prayer, or more than one — how should he act?

The answer: It has come in the book *al-Mujzi*, in the section on the ages of camels, cattle, and the like, that there is no tashriq except in a congregational city — that is, no prayer of the two festivals — because the two festival prayers are prayed at the rising of the sun, and the one who prays them is called المشرّق (al-mushriq). And it has come in *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf*: whoever forgets part of the magnification completes it before he bows; and if he has already passed beyond the bowing and is certain of the omission, he performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

The question: What do you say concerning the magnification from the day of Arafah to the last of the days of tashriq being twenty-three magnifications, while in our lands they add one magnification after the festival prayer so that it becomes twenty-four? And what is the meaning of this?

The answer: The magnification in those days is twenty-three, and it is a likeness of what obtains in the summons of the five speakers — the possessors of firm resolve — who correspond to the five prayers, drawn from the mention of the ten spiritual ranks and the ten bodily ranks, and the mention of the speaker of the cycle, his executor, and the Imam of the age.

> فَخُذْ مَا أُوتِيتَ وَكُنْ مِنَ الشَّاكِرِينَ

So take what you have been given, and be among the thankful.

From another source: whoever prays the festival prayer while owing a makeup of the dawn prayer, his festival prayer is valid and he does not repeat it; and likewise the funeral prayer.

From our master al-Hasan ibn Idris: whoever prays the festival prayer before bringing out the alms of the breaking of the fast does not repeat it, and he hands over the alms afterward. The washing for the two festivals is a practice of the Prophet. The first of its time is the breaking of dawn, and the last of it is the time of the sun's passing the zenith. Whoever prays the festival prayer before the sun passes the zenith, for an excuse, it is valid for him.

He says in the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf*: the washing for the two festivals is a practice of the Prophet; the first of its time is the breaking of dawn and the last of it is the time of the sun's passing the zenith.

He says in the book of reports: and the supplication of devotion is:

> اَللَّهُمَّ اغْفِرْ لِي وَارْحَمْنِي وَعَافِنِي وَاعْفُ عَنِّي فِي الدِّينِ وَالدُّنْيَا إِنَّكَ عَلَى كُلِّ شَيْءٍ قَدِيرٌ

O God, forgive me, have mercy on me, grant me well-being, and pardon me in religion and in the world. You are powerful over all things.

He says in it: if the people of the desert attend — and those travelers who choose to attend the festival — that is good, and there is merit in it for them. And from it: it is fitting for whoever is among the people of the desert, at two leagues from the city or less, to witness the festival.

He says in it: its congregation is five, one of them the Imam, as with the congregation of the Friday prayer; and where there is one who establishes it, it is an obligation like the obligation of Friday. Whoever misses it with the one who establishes it but catches the two sermons prays it as four as well. Whoever misses one cycle of the festival prayer and catches the second with the Imam adds to it another cycle with its magnifications. The practice regarding the magnification is that it begins after the completion of the fast, on the night of the breaking of the fast and on the day of the breaking of the fast, until he prays and finishes the prayer. Whoever prays the festival prayer raises his hands with every magnification. Whoever forgets part of the magnification completes it before he bows; and if he has passed beyond the bowing and is certain of the omission, he performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

He says in the book *al-Musannaf*: the festival prayer is not prayed at Mina. Likewise our master al-Qadi al-Numan ibn Muhammad said in *Tawil al-Daaim*; and it has been verified from our master Idris ibn Hasan that the festival prayer is not prayed there in congregation, but each one prays it by himself, one by one.

He says in the book of questions and answers mentioned at the outset: intercourse with one's wife is permitted on the night of the festival of the breaking of the fast and before the festival prayer.

Our master Jafar ibn Mansur al-Yaman said in the book *al-Rida al-Batin*: it is permitted after the festival of the breaking of the fast to fast on the following day and thereafter. And he says in it: it is not permitted after this festival — meaning the festival of the sacrifice — as it is permitted after the festival of the breaking of the fast.

Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad ibn al-Fadl said in the *Hidden Epistle*, concerning the prayer of the festival of the breaking of the fast: الفطر (al-fitr) in the language means appearance and beginning. One says تَفَطَّرَتِ الْأَرْضُ وَانْفَطَرَتْ (تَفَطَّرَتِ الْأَرْضُ وَانْفَطَرَتْ) — meaning the earth split open and what lay within it appeared and came forth. Likewise تَفَطَّرَتِ الشَّجَرَةُ (tafattarat al-shajarah) means that its hidden fruit appeared. And فَطَرَ نَابُ الْبَعِيرِ (fatara nab al-bair) means that it came up and appeared. In all these examples there runs a single sense: it is the day on which the command and the things that had been hidden come to light. From this, the breaking of the fast was named fitr, for fasting is silence and concealment. God the Exalted said in the story of Maryam:

> إِنِّى نَذَرْتُ لِلرَّحْمَنِ صَوْمًۭا فَلَنْ أُكَلِّمَ ٱلْيَوْمَ إِنسِيًّۭا

I have vowed a fast to the Most Merciful, so I shall not speak to any human being today.

That is: I have vowed silence and stillness, showing nothing of what is within me. So fitr is the appearance of secrets and the knowledge of hidden things. And His saying,

> وإذا السماء انفطرت

And when the sky is split open,

means that it was cleft and what was within it came forth. In another meaning, الْفِطْر is the new; and فَطَرَ اللَّهُ الْخَلْقَ (fatara Allah al-khalq) means the beginning of His fashioning of them. The day of the breaking of the fast is therefore the day of the manifestation of the command that had been veiled. Then he said: Whoever has fasted the days of the month of Ramadan, for him is the breaking of the fast, the festival, and joy; and whoever does not know the meaning of the reality of fasting and its limits has nothing of God, and he is as God, mighty and majestic, said:

> عَامِلَةٌ نَاصِبَةٌ تَصْلَى نَارًا حَامِيَةً

Toiling, weary, burning in a scorching fire.

The festival prayer has neither a call to prayer nor a call to stand. He then said: the sermon at the breaking of the fast comes after the prayer. He said: the recitation in it is aloud. He said: the prayer of the sacrifice is on the day of the sacrifice — the day of the pilgrimage. He then said: the fact that this sermon begins contrary to all other sermons is a sign that the command appears otherwise than it was in the cycles that have passed. Every sermon begins with praise, except the sermon of the day of the sacrifice, which begins with the four magnifications.

## 22. Forgetting, Doubting, and Beginning Again

I turn now to the subject of omission in the prayer.

In the question-and-answer book I named at the outset — one of whose arrangers set out following the order of the chapters of the Book of the Pillars of Islam — the following is asked:

Question: What do you say if someone makes room for another to pray, standing in for a man who does not know all the positions in the prayer that require the prostration of omission? Must that man perform the two prostrations of omission after completing every obligatory act about which something appears uncertain to him, or not?

The answer is: if it is established that he has indeed committed an omission, he prostrates for it; otherwise he does not.

Question: What do you say regarding the statement in the Book of Purity that whoever errs by not pronouncing the opening takbir — or omits it deliberately or out of ignorance — must repeat the prayer? O our master, if a man forgets to open the prayer after every two rakahs in the four-rakah and six-rakah prayers, does that invalidate his prayer or not?

The answer is: if he did not sit after each pair of rakahs, his prayer is invalidated.

Question: What do you say regarding the statement in the Book of Purity that whoever inadvertently adds to his prayer, even without sitting for the tashahhud, must repeat the prayer; and that if he adds an extra bowing he repeats the prayer, but if he adds an extra prostration he completes the prayer and performs the two prostrations of omission? What does this mean?

The answer is: if he adds a bowing and has not sat for the tashahhud, he must repeat the prayer. But if he has sat for the tashahhud and completed two rakahs — whether with an additional bowing or an additional prostration — he has done so in order.

Question: What do you say about a man who is praying behind an Imam and neglects one of the pillars of the prayer while following the Imam? How is his prayer?

The answer is: the Imam bears the responsibility for the omission, except in the case of the bowing and the opening takbir — and the first thing is that he should not neglect those.

Question: What do you say regarding the statement in religious law that whoever forgets a prostration should prostrate after delivering the salaam, when he remembers? O our master, how does he prostrate after having left the prayer?

The answer is: he prostrates if he remembers before delivering the salaam. If he has already left the prayer, he has no recourse.

Question: What does our master say about a man who prayed the obligatory prayer and recited it in reverse order, from bottom to top? Must he perform the two prostrations of omission or not?

The answer is: there is nothing wrong with that, and it is not obligatory upon him to perform the two prostrations of omission.

Question: A man prayed some of the obligatory Zuhr prayer and sat down and recited the first tashahhud, then prayed two more rakahs and recited the tashahhud again, then recalled that he had prayed only three rakahs. What should he do?

The answer is: he prays the rakah he missed after the salaam, reciting in it the Opening and a surah, because it is one of the first two rakahs. He then recites the tashahhud, delivers the salaam, and performs the two prostrations of omission.

Question: Whoever neglects the first tashahhud in the obligatory prayer but recites the final tashahhud, then remembers before delivering the salaam — must he perform the prostration of omission or not?

The answer is: there is no explicit text on this, and where there is no text it has not been stipulated that the worshipper must prostrate.

Question: Regarding the man who repeats a single surah in the obligatory prayer after the Opening — must he perform the prostration of omission or not?

The answer is: this too has not been stipulated.

Question: Regarding a man who omits one obligation — should he prostrate the two prostrations of omission? And if he omits three obligations, does he prostrate separately for each omission with two prostrations, or do two prostrations suffice for all of them?

The answer is: the prostration of omission is not multiplied.

Question: Regarding a man who prays behind an Imam and makes it clear that he intends in his prayer to be a follower — if the Imam delivers the salaam of the dhikr yet he did not intend the prayer, is his prayer valid or not?

The answer is: if the Imam intends the prayer on his behalf, then it is so; if he does not, the prayer lapses.

Question: What is transmitted from Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع), who said:

> مَنْ سَهَىٰ عَنِ الْقِرَاءَةِ فِي بَعْضِ الصَّلَاةِ فَقَرَأَ فِي مَا بَقِيَ مِنْهَا أَجْزَاَهُ ذَٰلِكَ فَإِنْ نَسِيَ الْقِرَاءَةَ كُلَّهَا وَأَتَمَّ الرُّكُوعَ وَالسُّجُودَ وَالتَّكْبِيرَ لَمْ يَكُنْ عَلَيْهِ الْإِعَادَةُ

This statement presents no difficulty.

Question: If one forgets to recite some of the Quran, or all of it, must he perform the two prostrations of omission?

The answer is: the two prostrations of omission are required, and he performs them.

Question: What of a man whom the Imam has preceded in some part of the prayer? Must the latecomer perform the two prostrations of omission that the Imam himself owed before the latecomer arrived?

The answer is: whoever enters the prayer with the Imam prostrates those two prostrations for his Imam's omission — even if the only part he caught with him before the salaam was the opening takbir.

Question: In the case of one who was preceded and prays part of the prayer with the Imam, then makes an error in what remains upon him — must he prostrate for the omission?

The answer is: if he omits something while praying on his own, he prostrates.

Question: The text states that

> الْأَعْمَالُ لَا تُقْبَلُ اِلَّا بِالنِّيَّاتِ

deeds are accepted only with intentions. So if a person neglects — when purifying himself, or when beginning his prayer, or when fasting, or when performing any of the duties of God — to contract the intention with the tongue while he intends the act in his heart, is his action valid?

The answer is: the intention resides in the heart, and what is said with the tongue is merely a verbal expression, as mentioned in the Pillars. If he has contracted the intention in his heart, his act is complete.

He said in the councils of instruction that doubt in prayer has its root in omission, which is inattention. It is a word that troubles many of those who occupy themselves with jurisprudence, so that they suppose doubt to be like omission — but it is not so. The one who commits an omission is certain of what he has omitted, while the doubter is not certain of what he doubts.

Concerning the prostration of omission, it is said in the reports that it is performed after the salaam, and that after it one recites a brief testimony:

> بِسْمِ اللَّهِ وَبِاللَّهِ وَأَشْهَدُ أَنْ لَا اِلَهَ إِلَّا اللَّهُ وَصَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَى مُحَمَّدٍ وَآلِهِ

In the name of God, and by God, and I bear witness that there is no god but God, and may God bless Muhammad and his family.

It is also said: whoever falls into doubt while praying and does not know whether he is in an obligatory prayer or a voluntary one — if he stood up for the prescribed prayer and then became inattentive afterward and supposed it to be voluntary, that prayer suffices him for the prescribed one and his inattention does no harm; but if he stood up for a voluntary prayer, then it is voluntary. It is said: whoever doubts and does not know whether he prayed Zuhr or Asr — if some of the time of Asr still remains, he prays it; but if the sun has set and he then doubts, no making up is due from him. It is said: if you doubt about the dawn prayer, pray it again; and if you doubt about the sunset prayer, pray it again. It is said: whoever commits omissions repeatedly in a single prayer owes nothing but the two prostrations of omission.

I find in the Mukhtasar al-Musannaf that if a man prays a four-rakah prayer and then, through inattention, rises for a fifth — if he had already recited the tashahhud in the fourth, he delivers the salaam while standing and departs; if he had not yet recited it, he sits, recites the tashahhud, and performs the two prostrations of omission. If he remembers that he rose to a fifth rakah only after he has bowed, and he had already recited the tashahhud in the fourth, his prayer is complete, and he joins to that rakah another as a voluntary prayer for himself; but if he had not recited the tashahhud in the fourth and did not sit for the length of the tashahhud, he repeats the prayer. Whoever forgets a prayer and does not know whether he prayed two, three, or four rakahs — and the Imam who prays aloud in what is prayed silently — has done ill, though his prayer is complete; and if he did that inattentively, the two prostrations of omission are upon him. If he is not an Imam but prays alone, and recites silently in what is recited aloud, or aloud in what is recited silently, nothing is due from him. Whatever holds concerning omission in the prayer of residence holds equally in the prayer of travel.

I find, in the questions put by certain of the ranks of the island of India — with their answers from certain of the summoners of the island of Yemen, may God sanctify all their souls — in the twenty-sixth issue: concerning the man who is inattentive in the recitation in the obligatory prayer and recites in the second rakah, after the first, a surah taken from above, which is not what is required in the obligatory prayer — are the two prostrations of omission upon him? The answer is: this has not been stipulated, and he should not do it deliberately. And in the first issue of that collection: the text states that deeds are accepted only with intentions — so if a person neglects, when purifying himself, or in his prayer, or in his fasting, or when performing any of the duties of God, to contract the intention with the tongue while he intends the act in his heart, is his action valid? And if he goes beyond that and abandons the intention of the tongue entirely, is his action valid? The answer is: the intention resides in the heart, and what is uttered on the tongue is merely a statement, as mentioned in the Pillars. If he has contracted the intention in his heart, his act is complete.

I find in the book on the interpretation of the Sharia that whoever misses a prayer or forgets it should pray it when he remembers it.

Our master, the owner of the Mustansiriyya councils, God's (ع), said in the ninth of them: Listen now to what we recite to you of the meaning of doubt and omission in the prayer. The places of omission in prayer are seven, in six of which the two prostrations of omission are proper. They are the two that rub down the nose (اَلْمُرْغِمَتَانِ), and they are so named because they rub down the nose of the devil when the corruption of forgetfulness is repaired by them. Of these:

1. Whoever is inattentive to a prostration prostrates when he remembers it, and the two prostrations of omission are upon him.
2. Whoever forgets to sit for the first tashahhud and stands for the third rakah, and remembers before he bows that he did not sit, sits and recites the tashahhud, and when he delivers the salaam he performs the two prostrations of omission.
3. If his inattention to the first tashahhud carries him on until he has bowed and prostrated, he completes his prayer, and the two prostrations of omission are upon him.
4. Whoever is inattentive to the second tashahhud and delivers the salaam owes the two prostrations of omission.
5. Whoever, through inattention, delivers the salaam after two rakahs when he is in a four-rakah prayer, prays two rakahs after them and performs the two prostrations of omission.
6. Whoever, through inattention, rises from the final tashahhud after completing it to a fifth rakah owes the two prostrations of omission.
7. Whoever is inattentive behind the Imam owes no prostration of omission, for the Imam bears that for him.

These are the seven places of omission in which the two prostrations are proper. As for doubt in prayer, it is a word that troubles many of those who occupy themselves with jurisprudence, so that they suppose it to be like omission — but it is not so. The one who commits an omission is certain of what he omitted, while the doubter is not certain of what he doubts. Doubt falls into twelve cases; in some of them there is a prostration of omission, and in others nothing at all. Those in which there is a prostration of omission are:

1. If the worshipper doubts about the bowing while he is still in the prayer, he bows, and the two prostrations of omission are upon him.
2. If he doubts and does not know whether he is in one rakah or two, he builds upon what is certain, and that is the lesser.
3. Likewise, if he doubts and does not know whether he has prayed two rakahs or three, he builds upon what is certain.
4. If he doubts and does not know whether he has prayed three or four, he delivers the salaam, prays two rakahs seated, and performs the two prostrations of omission.
5. If he does not know whether he has prayed two or four, he delivers the salaam, prays two rakahs standing, and performs the two prostrations of omission.

As for those in which nothing is due:

6. If the worshipper doubts about the opening takbir after he has bowed, he continues in his prayer and nothing is due from him.
7. Likewise if he doubts about the recitation after his bowing.
8. Likewise if he doubts about the bowing after his prostration.
9. Likewise if he doubts about the first prostration after his second prostration.
10. Likewise if he doubts about the prostration after the tashahhud.
11. Likewise if he doubts about anything of the prayer after the salaam.

In all of this nothing is due from him.

Our master, the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said in his four hundred manners:

> مَنْ كَانَ عَلَى يَقِينِهِ فَأَصَابَهُ شَكٌّ فَلْيَمْضِ عَلَى يَقِينِهِ فَاِنَّ الشَّكَّ لَا يَدْفَعُ الْيَقِينَ وَلَا يَنْقُصُهُ

Whoever is certain of something and is then struck by doubt should act on his certainty, for doubt neither dispels certainty nor diminishes it.

Among the questions submitted by certain shaykhs of India — may God be pleased with them — along with answers from our masters the summoners of Yemen — may God exalt their sanctity and grant us their intercession and their nearness — is the following. Question: What do you say concerning a man who is inattentive in forming his intention and does not declare himself a follower? Is his prayer valid or not? Answer: If his intention was to follow the Imam, his prayer is complete, even though he did not utter it aloud.

In the book of questions, I was asked about one who doubts during his prayer and does not know how much of it he has performed. He builds on what he is certain of and then prostrates the two prostrations of omission after he delivers the salaam. If, however, he is certain of nothing — not knowing whether he added to or fell short of his prayer — he begins again from the beginning, and so likewise does the Imam.

I now turn to what interrupts the prayer.

In the question-and-answer book named at the outset, arranged according to the chapters of the book of the Pillars of Islam: whoever vomits during the prayer, and the vomit reaches his garment, should wash the garment if water is close at hand; otherwise his prayer is void, and he then washes his garment and prays again. Whoever breaks his purity before the tashahhud must repeat the prayer; and if he breaks it after he has said,

> اَلسَّلَامُ عَلَيْكَ أَيُّهَا النَّبِيُّ وَرَحْمَةُ اللَّهِ وَبَرَكَاتُهُ اَلسَّلَامُ عَلَيْنَا وَعَلَى عِبَادِ اللَّهِ الصَّالِحِينَ

"Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy of God and His blessings; peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of God" — his prayer is permitted, and he does not repeat it.

I mentioned in the Book of Purity that if a person suffers a nosebleed or vomits while in prayer, and can wash away the blood and stop the bleeding, and rinse away the vomit, all without turning his face from the qibla, he does so and builds upon his prayer. If he does turn his face from the qibla, however, he must repeat the prayer from its beginning. The reports add: unless he detects the breaking of purity — by finding its smell, hearing its sound, or having another person hear it from him.

I also mentioned in the book on the interpretation of prayer that the Prophet (ع) said:

> ثَلَاثٌ تَقْطَعُ الصَّلَاةَ الْمَرْأَةُ وَالْحِمَارُ وَالْكَلْبُ الْأَسْوَدُ

Three things interrupt the prayer: a woman, a donkey, and a black dog. There is also the report that the Messenger of God (ع) was standing in prayer when a dog passed before him, then a donkey passed, then a woman passed while he was praying; and when he had finished he said:

> رَأَيْتُ الَّذِي رَأَيْتُمْ وَلَيْسَ يَقْطَعُ صَلَاةَ الْمُؤْمِنِ شَيْءٌ وَلَكِنِ ادْرَءُوا مَا اسْتَطَعْتُمْ

I saw what you saw, and nothing interrupts the prayer of a believer; but ward off what you are able to ward off.

Our master, the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said in his four hundred manners:

> لَا يَقْطَعُ الصَّلَاةَ التَّبَسُّمُ وَيَقْطَعُهَا الْقَهْقَهَةُ

Smiling does not interrupt the prayer, but loud laughter interrupts it.

It is mentioned in the book of questions — its question from the most honorable Shaykh Shimun ibn Muhammad Ghuri al-Hindi, and its answer from our master Badr al-Din Hasan ibn Idris, may God exalt their sanctity and grant us their intercession and their nearness. Question: What do you say about a man who entered the obligatory prayer with the opening takbir and recited, and then the Imam arrived and came forward while the man was still standing; and when the Imam bowed, the man bowed with him without renewing the opening takbir? What is the state of his prayer? The answer: he must repeat it.

I come now to the prayer of one who has been preceded in part of the prayer.

It is mentioned in the question-and-answer book named at the outset, arranged according to the chapters of the book of the Pillars of Islam. Question: What do you say about a worshipper who reaches the Imam while the Imam is in the final tashahhud of any of the five prayers, the latecomer intending in his prayer to follow, sitting while the Imam sits, until when the Imam delivers the salaam of his prayer the latecomer rises and prays? Has the latecomer attained the congregation, and is his prayer valid? The answer: if he reached the Imam in the tashahhud — which is the last of the prayer's limits — he intends the prayer as a follower, sits, and makes the supplication of turning toward God; and when the Imam delivers the salaam, he rises and prays his prayer, and he has attained the prayer of the congregation.

It is mentioned in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf: if a latecomer reaches the Imam in the bowing, he says the takbir for the opening and then the takbir for the bowing, and that rakah is counted for him; and if he fears missing it, a single takbir suffices for both the opening and the bowing. Whoever reaches the Imam having been preceded enters into whatever the Imam is in — if it is something that is counted, he builds upon it; if not, he is one performing a voluntary act. He prays what he has caught and makes up what escaped him.

It is said in the reports concerning an Imam whose purity is broken, who then puts forward a man who has been preceded: if that man does not know what the Imam preceded him with, he proceeds on his certainty until those behind him say the glorification to him, and then he reverts accordingly.

Our master and lord Muhammad ibn Fahd, may God exalt his sanctity, said in his noble epistle, which he titled The Broad Road of the Path of Guidance for the Rightly Guided, in the twentieth question: You asked also, may God grant you success in what pleases Him, about the latecomer who is unable to make the intention by uttering it with his tongue, and who enters with the Imam into whatever part of the prayer and its rakahs the Imam is in. The answer to you in this, by the granting of God — be He glorified — of what is correct in speech upon it, and its truth is thus: it has come in the books of the friends of God (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that if the latecomer reaches the Imam in any rakah of his prayer and the Imam has not yet raised his head from the bowing in it, and the latecomer says a single takbir and enters with the Imam into his prayer by it, then he has attained that rakah with the Imam, and that is so together with the contracting of the intention in his heart upon it.

## 23. Children, Travelers, and the Sick

I turn now to the age at which boys are commanded to pray, and to what that entails.

### The Time at Which Boys Are Commanded to Pray

Our master Amir al-Muminin Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) said in his four hundred counsels of manners:

> عَلِّمُوا صِبْيَانَكُمْ مَا يَنْفَعُكُمُ اللَّهُ بِهِ

Teach your boys that by which God will benefit you.

And he said in the same place:

> عَلِّمُوا صِبْيَانَكُمُ الصَّلَاةَ وَخُذُوهُمْ بِهَا إِذَا بَلَغُوا ثَمَانَ سِنِينَ

Teach your boys the prayer, and hold them to it when they reach eight years.

Our master the noble dai Ibrahim ibn Husayn al-Hamidi said in the book عِبَارَةُ الْبِشَارَةِ that Amir al-Muminin (ع) said:

> وَالصَّبِيُّ يَتَغَرُّ لِسَبْعِ سِنِينَ وَيَبْلُغُ لِأَرْبَعَ عَشَرَ وَيَكْمُلُ طُولُهُ وَعَرْضُهُ وَعُمْقُهُ لِإِحْدَى وَعِشْرِينَ سَنَةً وَيَكْمُلُ عَقْلُهُ لِثَمَانِيَةٍ وَعِشْرِينَ سَنَةً

The boy sheds his milk teeth at seven years, reaches maturity at fourteen, completes his height and breadth and depth at twenty-one years, and completes his intellect at twenty-eight years.

And in مُخْتَصَرُ الْآثَارِ I find this: they are to be separated in their sleeping places when they reach this stage, as a precaution against their proximity to one another. They have drawn close to maturity and to the stirring of desire, and there is concern that something to be feared may arise between them when one draws near another.

### The Prayer of the Traveler

In the book of questions and answers I mentioned above — arranged by one of its arrangers according to the order of the chapters of the book دَعَائِمُ الْإِسْلَامِ — I find:

Question: What do you say of a man who has two houses, one in Ahmadabad and the other in Nahrwala, and who lives some days in Ahmadabad and some days in Nahrwala? May he pray in full before he enters the boundaries of the town in which his house stands?

Answer: If he stays for five prayer times with his wife or with his mahram relatives by lineage, he prays in full and fasts.

Question: What do you say of what has come in the Book of Purity, that one whose journey is a pleasure outing does not shorten the prayer? Our master, what is meant by a journey of pleasure?

Answer: If he has land of the extent that would ordinarily require the shortening of the prayer, and he wishes to walk in it for pleasure, he does not shorten the prayer.

Question: What do you say of a man who prays the sunnah and the supererogatory prayer aboard a ship, and the ship turns? Must he turn with it or not?

Answer: He ascertains the direction at the moment he enters into the prayer. If the ship turns, nothing is required of him. If he is able to turn with it and does so, that is better. If he is in an obligatory prayer, let him turn with it as much as he is able.

Question: What do you say of an owned man who is authorized to trade, or who is not authorized, and whose master sends him traveling in the month of Ramadan or at another time? Must he shorten the prayer and break the fast or not?

Answer: Yes, he shortens and he breaks the fast.

Question: What do you say of the divorced women in India, when someone goes out with them on a journey for trade or for anything else, in the month of Ramadan or at another time? Does he pray in full because of their company, and does he fast the month of Ramadan, or not? And likewise if someone goes out with the collector?

Answer: If their purpose was to go out with him, they pray in full. If they went out for something other than that, their ruling is the ruling of travelers.

Question: What do you say of this, that God has forbidden in the Quran thirteen degrees of kinship in marriage? Our master, with which of these degrees does one travel, and through which of them does one complete the prayer and the fast?

Answer: Everyone who is of the blood kin is one through whom the traveler completes his prayer and his fast by his stopping for the five prayers in the place where that relative is. A man completes through his wife, but this does not hold through his wife's relatives, nor through those related by nursing.

Question: And one who travels from Nahrwala to Ahmadabad and meets one of his relatives outside the town, does he pray in full or not?

Answer: He does not pray in full until after he has entered the boundaries of the town in which his relative is.

From another place: whoever leaves his house for a journey and spends the night in the city, or in the house of a relative, or sits a day or more, does not shorten the prayer so long as he is in the city; and if he goes outside the city and spends the night or sits a day or more, he does not shorten the prayer. The master of the ship and the merchants all pray in full before one of them sets out from Cambay to Aden, and nothing further than this is required of them, nor is anything imposed upon him after he reaches his family.

From another place: whoever travels, and the time of prayer comes while the clouds are heavy so that the qibla is not known, prays each obligatory prayer toward the four directions, and God, may He be exalted, is the more excusing toward His servant when He afflicts him. If he prayed and finished, and then the sun broke through and he knew the qibla and there still remained time for the prayer to be performed, he repeats the prayer. If a traveler alights in a place intending to reside there, and after two days or more a matter arises for him in which travel is unavoidable, he does not repeat.

Whoever enters Mecca prays in full and does not shorten, even if he sits an hour or a day, because of the word of God:

> سَوَآءً ٱلْعَكِفُ فِيهِ وَٱلْبَادِ

The resident and the visitor stand equal.

Our master Ismail ibn Sayyidina Hiba held that the prayer is completed in full only within the sanctuary of the House of God, and that in Mecca the Honored, the prayer is shortened.

**Question:** What is your ruling on the shaykhs who have leave to travel among the villages — such as Shaykh Abdullah ibn Nuh, who moves among them visiting the believers, some villages being close together and others separated by a distance that requires shortening the prayer or more? Do they shorten the prayer and break the fast, or not?

**Answer:** Such a man is like the prince who makes his rounds within his emirate. He is not required to shorten the prayer or to break the fast. This answer comes from our master Idris.

**Question:** A traveler reaches his people before midday and stays with them until after midday of the second day. Does that count for him as a day and a night, or not?

**Answer:** If he intended to remain until he prays on the second day, his prayer and his fast are complete.

**Question:** As for the relatives through whom a traveler completes his prayer and fast — are these the relatives God, mighty and majestic, mentions in the Quran where He says:

> حُرِّمَتْ عَلَيْكُمْ أُمَّهَتُكُمْ وَبَنَاتُكُمْ وَأَخَوَتُكُمْ وَعَمَّتُكُمْ وَخَلَتُكُمْ وَبَنَاتُ ٱلْأَخِ وَبَنَاتُ ٱلْأُخْتِ

Forbidden to you are your mothers, your daughters, your sisters, your paternal aunts, your maternal aunts, the daughters of the brother, and the daughters of the sister.

And what corresponds to these verses among men? Do mothers-in-law, stepdaughters, and relatives through nursing also complete the prayer and fast for the traveler, or not?

**Answer:** The relatives of lineage — both male and female — are those through whom the prayer and fast are completed. As for mothers-in-law and those mentioned alongside them, no.

From another source: a man who enters the gate of a city or village after a journey during Ramadan before midday, but whose midday arrives before he enters his house, completes his prayer and his fast. Whoever travels and reaches a village where his wife's kinswoman or kinsman resides shortens the prayer. But if the woman has come to her father's house yet does not dwell there, and she has settled as a resident, or she is in the house of the mahram relative, and she is young and has not reached maturity, or she has given birth to that child, he prays in full.

Whoever travels less than two barids intending residence, and the time of prayer comes upon him on the road, prays in full and does not shorten. And whoever travels one barid intending to return, then returns to his family, and the time of prayer comes upon him on the road, prays in full and does not shorten.

**Question:** It has come in the shariah that when a man arrives from a journey and enters the road of his quarter, he prays in full. Our master, are Kanbur and Sarangpur and the rest of the outskirts of Ahmadabad all considered one with it, each within its boundary?

**Answer:** It is not permitted for a traveler returning from his journey to pray in full until he has entered his own house. But if he enters one of the city gates and has not yet reached his dwelling, and fears the prayer will pass, he prays shortened. Likewise, if he leaves his house while still within the city and has not yet passed through any of its gates, and the prayer falls due while he is in the city, he prays shortened — as has been set out plainly and expressly in the book مُخْتَصَرُ الْآثَارِ, the composition of our master al-Numan, where it is stated: the traveler breaks the fast and shortens when he goes out from his dwelling and sets out on the journey, until he returns and enters his dwelling, if he is pressing on in the journey. But if one of his blood kin meets him at a distance from his town short of the travel distance, and that blood relative is resident in that town, that traveler completes his prayer.

**Question:** If a man intends to reside in a lodging for ten days, then leaves it after four or five days because an unavoidable need has arisen, what is his state regarding the prayers and fasts he has performed?

**Answer:** So long as the intention of residence remains in his heart, he prays and fasts in full. When he intends to travel and depart from the place where he was residing, and sets out on the journey, he shortens from the moment that intention arises in his heart and he resolves upon it.

**Question:** If the traveler leaves a lodging in which he was residing — before the ten days are complete — for another lodging nearby, with no more than a mile between them, does he need to intend a full ten days of residence in the new lodging, or does his earlier intention stand complete with the fulfillment of the first ten days?

**Answer:** So long as he intends to return to the place in which he first intended residence, he prays and fasts in full. But if he left it with the intention of traveling, he does not pray in full in the other place unless he renews his intention and resides there ten days.

**Question:** If the traveler has family in a village and he enters its outskirts or a lodging within it, but has not yet come face to face with his family and relatives there, does he pray in full or shortened?

**Answer:** He does not pray in full until he meets his blood relative, provided he is staying with that blood relative for five prayer times — unless he intends to reside ten days; for when he reaches the village and intends residence, he prays and fasts in full.

These three answers are from our master Idris ibn Hasan.

It is said in the book مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ: if the traveler prays in full — whether forgetfully or deliberately — and time still remains, he must repeat the prayer; but if the time has passed, then no. Every prayer or prostration that becomes obligatory upon a person while he is on the ground cannot be discharged on horseback; and every prayer or prostration that becomes obligatory upon him while he is riding may be discharged on the ground. As for the Sacred Mosque, there is no shortening within it, the resident and the visitor being alike.

It is said in مُخْتَصَرُ الْآثَارِ: they said the traveler prays the sunnah and the supererogatory prayer; and if he is traveling by day and the journey presses him, he prays the obligatory prayer as two rakahs and defers the sunnah until he alights at night, then performs it together with the night prayer.

From the same book: the barid is four farsakhs; the farsakh is three miles; the mile is three thousand cubits, which is a thousand even paces of a single leg — from where the right foot is lifted to where it is set down. If the place a man intends is one barid away, and he has neither family nor dwelling there, and he intends to return within less than ten days, he shortens and breaks the fast until he departs. The traveler breaks the fast and shortens when he goes out from his dwelling and sets out on the journey, until he returns and enters his dwelling, if he is pressing on in the journey. But if one of his blood kin meets him at a distance from his town short of the travel distance, and that blood relative is resident in that town, that traveler completes his prayers.

In مُخْتَصَرُ الْآثَارِ, I find the ruling that praying on the beaten highway is forbidden. And in تَأْوِيلُ الدَّعَائِمِ, concerning the supererogatory prayer of the Messenger of God upon his mount while he was heading toward Medina, it is recorded that he would gesture with his head for the prostration, the qibla being behind him.

I also record here from the questions of certain of the hudud of the Indian island, with their answers from certain of the duat of the Yemeni island:

The thirty-fifth question: concerning the traveler who follows a resident imam, then gives the tashahhud after two rakahs and salutes while the imam is still completing the prayer — is the traveler's prayer sound or not? Answer: This is expressly stipulated: if he wishes, he may withdraw after two rakahs; and if he waits for the imam until he salutes, there is no harm in that.

Also from that source: Question: What do you say of the imam who leads the people in prayer aboard a ship while seated — because the ship's movement, or something else, makes standing impossible for some of them, while the rest are able? What is the state of their prayer? Answer: A dispensation has been granted for this on account of the ship, when they are unable. Whoever is able, however, should hasten and pray alone, standing.

Also from that source: Question: What do you say of the copy of the Quran and of other books — may they be used as a pillow in travel and at home, or not? For the common people narrate from the Prophet that he said:

> لَا تَوَسَّدُوا بِالْقُرْآنِ

Do not use the Quran as a pillow.

Answer: It ought to be raised upon the head, for the honor of the Quran and for good manners, but this is not obligatory.

Our master and mawlana Jafar ibn Sayyidina Sulayman said in غُرُفَاتُ الْجِنَانِ, in answer to questions from his servant, Sayyidina Ali ibn Sulayman, the author of this book, which he answered for him from the Yemeni island to the Indian island: And that which you asked about, saying, what do you say of one who shortens and prays behind a resident imam, and sits after his completion of the two rakahs while the imam rises to complete his prayer, and there is no one behind the imam except that one who shortens — does the imam say the takbir aloud in the remainder of his prayer or not? The answer is that nothing obliges the imam to say the takbir aloud; he should lower his voice with it. His raising the voice was for the sake of the one following him, so when no follower remains with him through his bowing, his prostration, and his rising, there is no sense in his raising the voice with it. Know that.

Our master Jafar ibn Mansur al-Yaman said in كِتَابُ الرِّضَاعِ الْبَاطِنِ: the sunnah of shortening applies to three prayers — the first, the afternoon, and the late evening. Each of these has four rakahs as its obligation, making twelve rakahs in total; the shortening from each four rakahs is two rakahs, so six rakahs in all are shortened.

I also record from the questions of the most noble Shaykh Hasan ibn Nuh to our master Idris ibn Hasan, with his answers to him:

The eleventh question: may a man travel with his son's wife, or with his wife's mother, without a screen, and speak with them? And may he bury her and travel with her? And likewise, what is the state of kin by nursing? Answer: There is no harm in it under necessity, if none of her blood kin is to be found — and God knows best. Yet the better course is caution regarding these women in the matters mentioned, because of the saying of the Prophet:

> اَلْمُتَّقِي مَنْ تَرَكَ مَا لَا بَأْسَ فِيهِ حَذَرًا أَنْ يَقَعَ فِي مَا بَأْسٌ فِيهِ

The God-fearing man is the one who leaves what carries no harm, out of caution against falling into what does.

This applies when one of their blood kin is available. When none is to be found, however, all of that is permitted — and God knows best what is right.

Our master Muhammad ibn Fahd stated in his treatise entitled لَاحِبَةُ سَبِيلِ الْهُدَى لِلْمُهْتَدِينَ:

The seventeenth question: You asked, my brother, about a traveler who covers the distance of a barid, resides there for eight days, and returns on the ninth. Does he shorten his prayer or pray it in full? Two rulings appear in the books of the purified shariah: first, that when a traveler covers a barid and returns within it, he shortens his prayer, because the outward and return journeys together make two barids for him; and second, that the limit of residence is ten days.

The answer — and may God the Glorified likewise grant you success toward what is right in it — is this: what is expressly stipulated in the books of the friends of God (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) regarding the residence mentioned is that its limit is ten days, just as you said, and neither less than that nor short of it. The better course for one who travels a barid and intends to return after some days is to commit to a stay of ten days and pray in full. Then, should it become clear to him that he wishes to return before those ten days are complete, he may do so; he is not obliged to complete the residence — I mean the ten days mentioned — as the texts in the books of the friends of God (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) make plain. His praying in full, as described, is not invalidated.

You also asked about the prayer of a traveler who leads residents as imam. He said that this is permitted when the imam is among the men of excellence: he gives the salutation after two rakahs, and those following him rise and complete their prayers. This is particular to the people of the high ranks, and does not extend to others among all the hudud.

You also asked about the prayer of one who has performed tayammum leading those who have performed the ablution. That is permitted for the people of the high ranks, as is established by what has been recorded concerning our master and mawlana, the Dai of the age, Jafar ibn Sayyidina Sulayman. When he was in the Yemeni island and descended from the fortress of Tayba to the land of Haraz, he purified himself for the noon prayer of one of his days as others purify themselves — but that did not suit him, because of the scrupulous care he took in this and in other matters, and scrupulous care in purification with the complete purification was not available to him. When he finished, he performed tayammum where no one perceived it, then led the people in congregation. Then he said to certain of the most noble hudud of his dawa: thus has the dispensation come for the prayer of one who has performed tayammum leading those who have performed the ablution, when some weighty matter makes it necessary for the good of the guiding dawa and the raising of its standard, just as there is the dispensation for the prayer of the traveler leading residents.

The book أُنْسُ الْأَفْئِدَةِ الْمُنِيبَةِ records that he — meaning Amir al-Muminin (ع) — ruled that six categories of people do not shorten their prayers or their fasts:

1. collectors who travel a circuit in their collecting;
2. merchants who travel from market to market in their trade;
3. princes who travel a circuit within their emirate;
4. men who go out in pursuit of game;
5. shepherds who seek out places where rain has fallen and the trees have sprouted;
6. warriors who cut the road.

The book of the question from the most noble Shaykh Shamun ibn Muhammad Ghuri al-Hindi, with the answer from our master Badr al-Din Hasan ibn Idris, records the following:

Question: What do you say regarding this matter? Certain shaykhs raised a question about the women who are mahram: with which of them does one travel, and with which of them does one not shorten the prayer by stopping with her for the five prayers? The answer given was that when the person is of blood kin, the traveler completes the prayer and the fast by stopping with that person for the five prayers; and that a man completes his prayer through his wife, but that this does not hold through the wife's relatives nor through kinship by nursing. So make clear to us, our master, in detail: with which of these women does one travel and touch with one's hand or otherwise, and with which of them does one not shorten the prayer? And explain the detail of the ruling that a man completes his prayer through his wife — for on this point the feet of the deniers have slipped, and deliverance comes from you, O best of those who consider and reflect upon the affairs of religion.

Answer: He does not pray in full except when one of his mahrams is stopping in a place and the traveler stops with that person for the five prayer times — then he prays in full. Likewise, he prays in full through his wife if she is resident, when he stops with her for the five prayer times.

The questions from certain shaykhs of India, with the answers from certain of our masters, the duat of Yemen, record the following:

Question: What do you say — does the resident lead the travelers in prayer, or does the traveler lead the residents?

Answer: If the traveler is more excellent than the residents, he leads them and withdraws after two rakahs, and they rise and complete the prayer. Likewise, if the resident is more excellent than the travelers, he leads them, and they withdraw after two rakahs.

### The Prayer of the Sick

The book of questions and answers mentioned above — arranged by one of its arrangers according to the order of the chapters of the book دَعَائِمُ الْإِسْلَامِ — records the following:

Question: What do you say of the sick man who is unable to pray standing, nor seated cross-legged with the bowing and prostration? How should he proceed?

Answer: He prays seated, or cross-legged with the bowing and prostration, for God is the more excusing toward His servant when He afflicts him.

The Book of Purity states: if he is not able to gesture with his head, he gestures with his eyes; and if he is not able to do that, he gestures with his heart.

مُخْتَصَرُ الْآثَارِ states: if the sick man is not able to pray anything but the obligatory prayer, he prays it; then when he recovers, he may make up, if he wishes, what he omitted of the sunnah or the supererogatory prayer — and in that there is merit and reward, though making it up is not obligatory as the making up of the obligatory prayer is obligatory.

مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ states: whoever drinks something by which he treats himself, and it brings about the loss of his reason, must repeat the prayers that passed him by while he was in that state. The same applies if he does something to himself that brings about the loss of his reason — and it makes no difference whether, in what he did, he was obedient to God, the Glorified and Exalted, or disobedient.

## 24. Eclipse, Fear, Rain, and the Night Vigil

I turn now to the prayer of the eclipse. What follows is drawn from the book of questions and answers mentioned earlier, which one of its arrangers organized according to the chapters of كِتَابُ دَعَائِمِ الْإِسْلَامِ (The Pillars of Islam).

**Question:** Concerning the eclipse that occurred in the year eight hundred and thirty-seven, in the month of Dhu al-Qadah: it began on Monday, two hours after the sun had passed its height, and the solar eclipse fell out in accordance with the reckoning of the table. Yet the letters of the Brethren of Purity, in the treatise on astronomy, state that a solar eclipse occurs only at the end of the month, and the cause of this is given there. This has happened repeatedly in years before this one. We were fasting during the first days of the blessed month of Ramadan when an eclipse occurred, and the believers were left perplexed about how the matter is to be answered.

**Answer:** A statement has come from Mawlana, the author of the letters (ع), in summary, and the pure Imams and the ranks that follow them have explained it. They said that the eclipse of the moon occurs at the dawn of the fifteenth and at the dawn of the sixteenth, while the eclipse of the sun may occur at the beginning of the month or at its end. The matter is in the hand of God.

> وَلَا حَوْلَ وَلَا قُوَّةَ إِلَّا بِاللَّهِ الْعَلِيِّ الْعَظِيمِ

There is no might and no power except with God, the Most High, the Great.

**Question:** One who joins the imam at the third rakah counts it as the beginning of his own prayer. He bows with the imam's bowing and prostrates with his prostration. When the imam rises from the prostration, he rises with him and prays the five bowings with him and prostrates with him. When the imam makes the testimony, the latecomer sits poised and ready. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer rises and completes what remains upon him of the bowings; he recites the qunut, bows, prostrates, makes the testimony, and gives the salutation. He does not count the first two prostrations he performed with the imam, just as is done by one who catches only the prostration at the end of the obligatory prayers with the imam.

Another case: whoever misses the eclipse prayer with the imam counts his joining as the beginning of his own prayer. If he comes before the first two prostrations, he prays what remains with the imam, and whenever the imam prostrates in the first two prostrations, he prostrates with him — for it has been mentioned that one who leaves these is counted as unable. When the imam rises, the latecomer prays the five bowings with him and prostrates and rises with the imam; when the imam prostrates, he prostrates with him; when the imam sits, the latecomer sits poised and ready; and when the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer rises and prays what remains upon him of the bowings, then prostrates, makes the testimony, and gives the salutation. He does not count the first two prostrations in which he prostrated with the imam, just as is done by one who has been outstripped in the prayer and catches only the prostration and the obligatory portions.

It is said in the book of purity: the eclipse prayer is not to be prayed until the eclipse appears and is seen. It is not prayed on the word of an astrologer or of anyone else that it is occurring, and if a veil of cloud or anything else intervenes, it is not prayed until the eclipse appears and is seen with the eye.

I turn now to the prayer of fear, and again from that same book of questions and answers.

**Question:** What do you say concerning what has come in the book of purity — that when there is the crossing of swords, close fighting, and the thrusting of spears, they make their prayers into takbir, so that they say one takbir in the place of each bowing? O our master, do they shorten the takbir as they shorten in the prayer of fear? **Answer:** He shortens in the takbir as shortening has been made obligatory in it.

It is said in the book مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ: if the imam prays the prayer of fear with three men while three men stand guard for him, that is permitted. If their number is fewer than three, it is not recommended for them to pray the prayer of fear, since the word "party" does not apply to fewer than three. And if the eclipse prayer and a funeral prayer come together for the imam along with fear, he prays over the funeral, and prays the eclipse prayer and the prayer of fear.

I turn now to the prayer for rain. It is said in كِتَابُ الْاِقْتِصَارِ, and in الأخبار, and in اَلْمُنْتَخَبَةُ: then he faced the people and said one hundred takbirs; then he turned to his right and glorified God one hundred times; then he turned to his left and declared the oneness of God one hundred times, raising his voice in all of this. Then he faced the people and praised God with one hundred utterances of praise and magnification, and extolled Him. It is said in the book of purity: he delivered two sermons in the same manner as is done for the two feasts; then he descended and departed, and the people departed. He delivers the sermon leaning upon an Arabian bow, if he finds one, as the Messenger of God (ع) did. And from the same source: if they go out on a day other than Monday, there is no harm in it. It is said in مُخْتَصَرُ الْمُصَنَّفِ: whoever delivers the sermon before the prayer must pray and then deliver the sermon.

I turn now to the witr prayer, the two bowings of dawn, and the qunut, and again from that book of questions and answers.

**Question:** Concerning a man who is heedless of the qunut. **Answer:** In the witr he must repeat, and in the dawn prayer he performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

**Question:** What do you say concerning a man who prays the witr and forgets the qunut, then remembers it after he has bowed or prostrated or sat? Does he repeat the witr, or does he perform the two prostrations of forgetfulness? **Answer:** The qunut is a supplication that is not to be abandoned deliberately. Whoever forgets it need not repeat, and there are no two prostrations of forgetfulness in the sunnah prayers. Whoever abandons it in the witr deliberately must repeat. The least of the qunut is three glorifications or three takbirs.

**Question:** When is the qunut recited in the witr? **Answer:** The qunut is recited after the worshipper says:

> رَبَّنَا لَكَ الْحَمْدُ

Our Lord, to You belongs all praise.

Let that be verified. And whoever prays the shaf prayer of two bowings and then occupies himself with something until dawn breaks repeats the shaf as a make-up. If he has prayed three bowings and only the sitting remains until dawn breaks, he does not repeat the three, but makes up the sitting with the intention of a make-up.

**Question:** Concerning the worshipper who gives the salutation after the first bowing of the witr — is speech permitted to him, or does he join the consecration to the consecration? For it has been said that the prayer of one seated is half the prayer of one standing, and it has been said in another place:

> أَنَّ الصَّلَاةَ تَحْرِيمُهَا تَكْبِيرُهَا وَتَحْلِيلُهَا تَسْلِيمُهَا

الصَّلَاةُ تَحْرِيمُهَا التَّكْبِيرُ وَتَحْلِيلُهَا التَّسْلِيمُ — the prayer: its consecration is its takbir, and its release is its salutation. By which of the two, then, does one act? Answer: our master the Judge al-Numan, may God raise his sanctity, has mentioned in اَلْمُنْتَخَبَةُ أَنْ:

> وَالْوِتْرُ قَدْ يُفْصَلُ بِالسَّلَامِ
> مِنْ غَيْرِ أَعْمَالٍ وَلَا كَلَامٍ

The witr may be divided by the salutation, without actions and without speech. And if one is compelled to speak, there is no harm in it so long as the talk is not prolonged.

**Question:** Is it permissible to perform the shaf and the witr in their proper time during the first dawn — which the Arabs call ذَنَبُ السِّرْحَانِ — before the second dawn spreads across the sky, or not? And does the dispensation extend to praying the two bowings of the sunnah of dawn? **Answer:** The shaf prayer and the witr are performed in their proper time so long as the dawn has not spread; this is established.

Whoever prays the dawn prayer with the imam and bows before the imam, then raises his head and bows a second time with the imam, his prayer is valid, because his prayer follows the prayer of the imam. But if he prayed alone, he repeats the prayer. Whoever prays the witr and forgets the qunut performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness after the salutation.

**Question:** If a man prays the shaf, then enters the mosque and prays the greeting of the mosque during the time of the shaf, does he repeat the shaf or not? **Answer:** He repeats the shaf, because it is not for him to pray after it until dawn breaks. When dawn has broken he prays the greeting, then prays the dawn prayer.

It is said in مَعَالِمُ الدِّينِ: the witr prayer is obligatory, and there is no dispensation in abandoning it; it is among the appendages of the obligatory prayers, even though it is a sunnah.

It is said in the book of purity: he recites in the first bowing the opening of the Book and the surah:

> قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ

In the second bowing, I recite the opening of the Book and the surah:

> قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلنَّاسِ

Say: I seek refuge with the Lord of mankind.

I then make the testimony and give the salutation. These two bowings are part of the witr. I sit cross-legged throughout both of them, as already described, spreading my palms upon my thighs with my fingertips upon my knees, and I recite. When I have finished the recitation, I bow and place the inner surfaces of my palms upon my knees, still seated cross-legged. When I raise my head from the bowing, I draw my right foot back behind me, set my feet upright, prostrate, and then sit for the testimony as one sits in prayer.

Our master Muhammad ibn Fahd, may God raise his sanctity, said in his noble treatise, which he named لَاحِبَةُ سَبِيلِ الْهُدَى لِلْمُهْتَدِينَ, in the twenty-second question: you also asked about one who prays the shaf and the witr, then sits afterward for as long as is easy for him, then rises and prays the dawn — its sunnah and its obligation — and it afterward becomes clear to him that he was still in the night and the dawn had not yet come. What is his condition in that prayer? The answer is that his shaf and his witr stand confirmed, and he must repeat the dawn, both its sunnah and its obligation, unless the dawn was close upon him, in which case the sunnah prayer also stands confirmed for him. This is because it has come in the books of the friends of God (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that it is permissible to pray it before dawn, on account of the noble meaning beneath this that permits it. So know this.

The Commander of the Faithful, 'Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said in his four hundred points of conduct:

> تَوَكَّلُوا عَلَى اللَّهِ عِنْدَ رَكْعَتَيِ الْفَجْرِ بَعْدَ فَرَاغِكُمْ مِنْهَا فَفِيهَا تُعْطَى الرَّغَايِبُ

Place your trust in God at the two bowings of dawn after you have finished them, for in them the things most desired are granted.
